Of course. Here are 250 WAEC standard multiple-choice questions with answers and detailed explanations, generated from the provided textbook chapters.

---

### **Part I: Foundations of Civic Education**

**Chapter 1: Meaning and Scope of Government and Governance**

1.  The formal institutions, structures, and processes through which a state is ruled is known as...
    A) Governance
    B) Politics
    C) Government
    D) Constitution
    E) Sovereignty

    **Answer:** C. Government
    **Explanation:** Government refers to the specific, formal institutions (legislature, executive, judiciary) and the body of people that administer a state. Governance is a broader process of decision-making, while politics is the struggle for power.

2.  Which of the following best describes "governance"?
    A) The machinery or apparatus of the state.
    B) The process of decision-making and its implementation involving state and non-state actors.
    C) The struggle for power and allocation of scarce resources.
    D) The set of rules and regulations written in a single document.
    E) The legitimate use of force within a territory.

    **Answer:** B. The process of decision-making and its implementation involving state and non-state actors.
    **Explanation:** Governance is a broader concept than government. It focuses on the processes, relationships, and interactions between government institutions, civil society, the private sector, and citizens in managing public affairs.

3.  Harold Lasswell famously defined politics as...
    A) the art of the possible.
    B) who gets what, when, and how.
    C) the management of public affairs.
    D) the monopoly on the legitimate use of force.
    E) the activity of running for office.

    **Answer:** B. who gets what, when, and how.
    **Explanation:** This classic definition by political scientist Harold Lasswell captures the essence of politics as the process of determining the allocation of societal resources and power.

4.  The most fundamental function of any government is often considered to be the...
    A) provision of social welfare.
    B) management of the national economy.
    C) maintenance of law and order.
    D) construction of infrastructure.
    E) political socialization of citizens.

    **Answer:** C. maintenance of law and order.
    **Explanation:** Without security and order (both internal and external), other functions of government become impossible. It is the primary reason societies form governments, according to many political philosophers.

5.  Fiscal policy, a key function of economic management, involves the government's management of...
    A) money supply and interest rates.
    B) international trade and tariffs.
    C) taxation and public spending.
    D) public utilities and corporations.
    E) national defense and security.

    **Answer:** C. taxation and public spending.
    **Explanation:** Fiscal policy is the use of government revenue collection (mainly taxes) and expenditure (spending) to influence a country's economy. Monetary policy, on the other hand, deals with money supply and interest rates.

6.  A government where supreme power is vested in the people and exercised through representation is a...
    A) Monarchy
    B) Oligarchy
    C) Theocracy
    D) Democracy
    E) Dictatorship

    **Answer:** D. Democracy
    **Explanation:** Democracy, which means "rule by the people," is characterized by popular sovereignty, free and fair elections, and the protection of rights.

7.  In an absolute monarchy, the monarch's power is...
    A) limited by a constitution.
    B) shared with an elected parliament.
    C) derived from the people.
    D) ceremonial in nature.
    E) unrestricted by law.

    **Answer:** E. unrestricted by law.
    **Explanation:** An absolute monarch holds supreme, unchecked power, unlike a constitutional monarch whose powers are limited by a constitution and exercised by an elected government.

8.  "Rule by the few," often based on wealth or military control, is termed...
    A) Anarchy
    B) Oligarchy
    C) Democracy
    D) Monarchy
    E) Theocracy

    **Answer:** B. Oligarchy
    **Explanation:** Oligarchy is a form of government where power rests with a small, elite segment of society, distinguished by factors like wealth (plutocracy), family ties (aristocracy), or military control (junta).

9.  The principle of good governance that requires government operations and decision-making to be open to public scrutiny is...
    A) Accountability
    B) Participation
    C) Transparency
    D) Rule of Law
    E) Equity

    **Answer:** C. Transparency
    **Explanation:** Transparency means that information about government decisions and actions is freely available and accessible to those affected, allowing for public scrutiny.

10. Which principle of good governance ensures that all individuals, including the government itself, are subject to the law?
    A) Responsiveness
    B) Rule of Law
    C) Consensus Oriented
    D) Participation
    E) Effectiveness

    **Answer:** B. Rule of Law
    **Explanation:** The Rule of Law dictates that no one is above the law. It requires legal frameworks to be fair, equally enforced, and independently adjudicated.

11. The ability to influence the behavior of others is a central concept in politics known as...
    A) Authority
    B) Legitimacy
    C) Power
    D) Sovereignty
    E) Governance

    **Answer:** C. Power
    **Explanation:** Power is the core concept in politics, representing the capacity to affect outcomes and influence others' actions. Authority is legitimized power.

12. A system of government where religious leaders rule in the name of God is a...
    A) Plutocracy
    B) Gerontocracy
    C) Theocracy
    D) Meritocracy
    E) Technocracy

    **Answer:** C. Theocracy
    **Explanation:** Theocracy is a system of government where priests or religious figures rule, and religious law is the basis of the legal system.

13. An extreme form of authoritarianism where the state attempts to control all aspects of public and private life is...
    A) Totalitarianism
    B) Anarchy
    C) Liberalism
    D) Oligarchy
    E) Constitutional Monarchy

    **Answer:** A. Totalitarianism
    **Explanation:** Totalitarianism is the most extreme form of authoritarian rule, characterized by a pervasive ideology, total state control, and the suppression of all dissent.

14. The concept of "accountability" in governance means that officials are...
    A) always elected by the people.
    B) answerable for their actions and decisions.
    C) free to make decisions without interference.
    D) part of a permanent civil service.
    E) responsive to the majority party only.

    **Answer:** B. answerable for their actions and decisions.
    **Explanation:** Accountability is the principle that public officials must be held responsible for their conduct and performance, and there are mechanisms for the public to enforce this.

15. Building roads, providing education, and operating public hospitals are examples of which function of government?
    A) Maintenance of law and order
    B) National defense
    C) Economic management
    D) Provision of public services and social welfare
    E) Political socialization

    **Answer:** D. Provision of public services and social welfare
    **Explanation:** These are essential public services that benefit society as a whole and are typically provided or managed by the government.

**Chapter 2: Political Ideologies and Systems**

16. Which political ideology emphasizes individual liberty, rights, and the consent of the governed?
    A) Socialism
    B) Conservatism
    C) Nationalism
    D) Liberalism
    E) Communism

    **Answer:** D. Liberalism
    **Explanation:** Liberalism's core tenets are individual freedom, equality of opportunity, reason, and government by consent, often within a framework of constitutionalism and rule of law.

17. Conservatism is an ideology that places a high value on...
    A) radical change and revolution.
    B) collective ownership of property.
    C) tradition, authority, and social order.
    D) individual liberty above all else.
    E) the abolition of the state.

    **Answer:** C. tradition, authority, and social order.
    **Explanation:** Conservatism advocates for preserving established institutions and values, promoting social stability, and favoring gradual, pragmatic change over radical transformation.

18. An ideology that aims for a classless, stateless society where the means of production are communally owned is...
    A) Fascism
    B) Liberalism
    C) Anarchism
    D) Communism
    E) Conservatism

    **Answer:** D. Communism
    **Explanation:** Communism, in its theoretical form as envisioned by Karl Marx, is a radical branch of socialism that seeks to eliminate class distinctions, private property, and the state itself.

19. Nationalism promotes the interests of a particular...
    A) social class.
    B) religious group.
    C) political party.
    D) nation or ethnic group.
    E) economic system.

    **Answer:** D. nation or ethnic group.
    **Explanation:** Nationalism is an ideology centered on the nation, emphasizing national identity, unity, loyalty, and the pursuit of national sovereignty and self-governance.

20. A referendum is a mechanism of...
    A) representative democracy.
    B) direct democracy.
    C) authoritarianism.
    D) constitutional monarchy.
    E) one-party rule.

    **Answer:** B. direct democracy.
    **Explanation:** A referendum allows citizens to vote directly on a specific law or policy, which is a key feature of direct democracy where people participate in decision-making without intermediaries.

21. In a unitary system of government, sovereign power is...
    A) divided between central and regional governments.
    B) concentrated in a single central government.
    C) held by the legislative branch only.
    D) vested in independent local councils.
    E) shared equally among all citizens.

    **Answer:** B. concentrated in a single central government.
    **Explanation:** A unitary system features a strong central government that holds ultimate authority. Any regional or local units derive their powers from and are subordinate to the central government. France and the UK are examples.

22. A key feature of a federal system of government is...
    A) the fusion of executive and legislative powers.
    B) a single, uniform legal system for the entire country.
    C) a constitutional division of powers between central and state governments.
    D) the absence of a written constitution.
    E) the appointment of regional governors by the president.

    **Answer:** C. a constitutional division of powers between central and state governments.
    **Explanation:** Federalism is defined by the sharing of sovereignty between at least two levels of government (central/federal and regional/state), with this division of powers enshrined in the constitution. Nigeria and the USA are examples.

23. In a presidential system of government, the executive is...
    A) drawn from and accountable to the legislature.
    B) headed by a monarch.
    C) elected independently of the legislature for a fixed term.
    D) chosen by the leader of the majority party.
    E) fused with the legislative branch.

    **Answer:** C. elected independently of the legislature for a fixed term.
    **Explanation:** The presidential system is characterized by a clear separation of powers, where the President (head of executive) is separately elected and not directly accountable to the legislature in the way a Prime Minister is.

24. A major advantage of a parliamentary system is that...
    A) it prevents the executive from becoming too powerful.
    B) it guarantees political stability at all times.
    C) deadlocks between the executive and legislature are less likely.
    D) the head of state is always a monarch.
    E) elections are held at fixed, unchangeable intervals.

    **Answer:** C. deadlocks between the executive and legislature are less likely.
    **Explanation:** Because the executive (Prime Minister and cabinet) is drawn from and commands the confidence of the majority in the legislature, there is a fusion of power that typically allows for more efficient lawmaking and reduces the risk of gridlock seen in presidential systems.

25. The primary function of an opposition party in a democracy is to...
    A) destabilize the government.
    B) refuse to cooperate on all legislation.
    C) provide a check on the government and offer alternative policies.
    D) represent foreign interests.
    E) merge with the ruling party.

    **Answer:** C. provide a check on the government and offer alternative policies.
    **Explanation:** The role of a "loyal opposition" is crucial for accountability. It scrutinizes the government's actions, holds it accountable, and presents itself as a viable alternative government-in-waiting.

26. A political system with three or more parties having a realistic chance of gaining power is a...
    A) one-party system.
    B) two-party system.
    C) multi-party system.
    D) dominant-party system.
    E) no-party system.

    **Answer:** C. multi-party system.
    **Explanation:** Multi-party systems are characterized by competition among multiple parties, often leading to coalition governments. Germany and Italy are examples.

27. A major disadvantage of a one-party system is the...
    A) high cost of conducting elections.
    B) instability of coalition governments.
    C) lack of voter choice and accountability.
    D) slow pace of decision-making.
    E) difficulty in identifying a clear winner.

    **Answer:** C. lack of voter choice and accountability.
    **Explanation:** In a one-party system, citizens have no alternative political choices, which eliminates a key mechanism for holding the government accountable and can lead to authoritarianism.

28. The set of proposals and policies a political party presents to the electorate is known as its...
    A) constitution.
    B) ideology.
    C) party platform or manifesto.
    D) pressure group.
    E) caucus.

    **Answer:** C. party platform or manifesto.
    **Explanation:** The manifesto outlines the party's vision, goals, and specific policies it intends to implement if it wins the election.

29. The United States is a classic example of a...
    A) multi-party system.
    B) two-party system.
    C) one-party system.
    D) dominant-party system.
    E) parliamentary system.

    **Answer:** B. two-party system.
    **Explanation:** Although other parties exist, American politics is overwhelmingly dominated by the Democratic and Republican parties, which alternate in power.

30. A semi-presidential system, like that of France, features...
    A) a monarch as head of state and a president as head of government.
    B) a directly elected president with significant powers and a prime minister accountable to the legislature.
    C) a president who is also the leader of the legislature.
    D) a fusion of all three branches of government.
    E) the absence of a legislative branch.

    **Answer:** B. a directly elected president with significant powers and a prime minister accountable to the legislature.
    **Explanation:** A semi-presidential system is a hybrid that combines a powerful, directly elected president with a prime minister and cabinet who are responsible to the parliament.

---

### **Part II: Constitutional Framework and Institutions**

**Chapter 3: Constitutions and the Rule of Law**

31. A constitution is best defined as the...
    A) collection of all laws passed by a legislature.
    B) set of fundamental principles and rules by which a state is governed.
    C) document outlining a political party's platform.
    D) decisions made by the highest court of the land.
    E) unwritten traditions of a country.

    **Answer:** B. set of fundamental principles and rules by which a state is governed.
    **Explanation:** A constitution is the supreme law that establishes the framework of government, defines the powers of its branches, and outlines the relationship between the state and its citizens.

32. A country whose constitution is found in a variety of sources like statutes, conventions, and judicial decisions has a/an...
    A) rigid constitution.
    B) written constitution.
    C) unwritten constitution.
    D) federal constitution.
    E) flexible constitution.

    **Answer:** C. unwritten constitution.
    **Explanation:** An unwritten (or uncodified) constitution is not contained in a single document but is derived from multiple sources. The United Kingdom is the primary example.

33. A key advantage of a written constitution is its...
    A) flexibility.
    B) simplicity.
    C) clarity and accessibility.
    D) reliance on tradition.
    E) ease of amendment.

    **Answer:** C. clarity and accessibility.
    **Explanation:** By setting out the fundamental rules in a single, formal document, a written constitution provides a clear, accessible, and authoritative standard for governance.

34. A rigid constitution is one that...
    A) cannot be changed under any circumstances.
    B) can be amended by a simple majority in the legislature.
    C) is unwritten and based on conventions.
    D) requires a special and complex procedure for amendment.
    E) applies only to the central government.

    **Answer:** D. requires a special and complex procedure for amendment.
    **Explanation:** Rigidity refers to the difficulty of the amendment process, which is designed to protect fundamental principles from being easily changed. This often involves supermajorities or referendums.

35. The principle of Separation of Powers advocates for the division of governmental authority among the...
    A) central, state, and local governments.
    B) monarchy, aristocracy, and commoners.
    C) legislature, executive, and judiciary.
    D) political parties, interest groups, and media.
    E) military, police, and civil service.

    **Answer:** C. legislature, executive, and judiciary.
    **Explanation:** First articulated by Montesquieu, this principle aims to prevent tyranny by dividing the core functions of law-making (legislature), law-enforcing (executive), and law-interpreting (judiciary) into distinct and independent branches.

36. Which of the following is an example of "checks and balances"?
    A) The legislature making a law.
    B) The executive enforcing a law.
    C) The judiciary interpreting a law.
    D) The executive vetoing a bill passed by the legislature.
    E) The civil service implementing a policy.

    **Answer:** D. The executive vetoing a bill passed by the legislature.
    **Explanation:** Checks and balances are mechanisms that allow each branch of government to limit the power of the others. A presidential veto is a classic check by the executive on the legislative branch.

37. Fundamental Rights are usually enshrined in a constitution to...
    A) increase the power of the government.
    B) protect citizens from arbitrary state action.
    C) outline the duties of the legislature.
    D) regulate political parties.
    E) generate revenue for the state.

    **Answer:** B. protect citizens from arbitrary state action.
    **Explanation:** A Bill of Rights or Chapter on Fundamental Rights guarantees basic freedoms and protections for individuals, limiting what the government can do to them.

38. The power of a court to declare a law or executive action unconstitutional is known as...
    A) judicial review.
    B) judicial activism.
    C) legislative oversight.
    D) executive order.
    E) constitutional amendment.

    **Answer:** A. judicial review.
    **Explanation:** Judicial review is a crucial power of the judiciary in many systems, allowing it to act as the ultimate guardian of the constitution by striking down actions that violate it.

39. The primary function of the legislature is to...
    A) interpret laws.
    B) execute laws.
    C) make laws.
    D) appoint judges.
    E) conduct foreign policy.

    **Answer:** C. make laws.
    **Explanation:** The legislature (e.g., Parliament, Congress, National Assembly) is the law-making branch of government.

40. In a constitutional system, the "rule of law" implies that...
    A) the ruler's word is law.
    B) laws can be applied retroactively.
    C) the executive is above the law.
    D) government actions must be based on and subject to the law.
    E) only the legislature is bound by the constitution.

    **Answer:** D. government actions must be based on and subject to the law.
    **Explanation:** The rule of law means everyone, including government officials and institutions, is accountable under the law, which should be publicly known, equally enforced, and independently adjudicated.

41. A constitution that can be amended by the same process as ordinary law is described as...
    A) rigid.
    B) federal.
    C) written.
    D) flexible.
    E) unitary.

    **Answer:** D. flexible.
    **Explanation:** A flexible constitution allows for easier adaptation to changing circumstances because its amendment process is the same as for regular legislation (e.g., a simple majority vote). The UK constitution is an example.

42. The "due process" of law, a fundamental right, primarily relates to the right to...
    A) vote in elections.
    B) freedom of speech.
    C) a fair hearing in legal proceedings.
    D) own property.
    E) practice any religion.

    **Answer:** C. a fair hearing in legal proceedings.
    **Explanation:** Due process encompasses the procedural guarantees that ensure fairness in the justice system, such as the presumption of innocence, right to counsel, and right to a public trial.

43. Customs, judicial precedents, and historical charters are major sources of a/an...
    A) written constitution.
    B) codified constitution.
    C) rigid constitution.
    D) unwritten constitution.
    E) federal constitution.

    **Answer:** D. unwritten constitution.
    **Explanation:** These sources are characteristic of an unwritten (uncodified) constitution, where the fundamental rules are not contained in one document but spread across various statutes, court decisions, and conventions.

44. The doctrine of separation of powers is most strictly applied in a...
    A) parliamentary system.
    B) presidential system.
    C) monarchical system.
    D) unitary system.
    E) confederal system.

    **Answer:** B. presidential system.
    **Explanation:** The presidential system, with its separately elected executive and legislature and independent judiciary, is designed around a formal separation of personnel and powers, unlike the parliamentary system which features a fusion of executive and legislative branches.

45. The chapter of a constitution that details the rights of citizens is often called the...
    A) Preamble.
    B) Bill of Rights.
    C) Amendment Clause.
    D) Supremacy Clause.
    E) Appendix.

    **Answer:** B. Bill of Rights.
    **Explanation:** The Bill of Rights is the specific section of a constitution that enumerates and guarantees the fundamental rights and freedoms of the people.

**Chapter 4: Executive Branch and Administrative Structures**

46. The executive branch of government is primarily responsible for...
    A) making laws.
    B) interpreting laws.
    C) implementing and enforcing laws.
    D) amending the constitution.
    E) selecting legislators.

    **Answer:** C. implementing and enforcing laws.
    **Explanation:** The executive is the "action arm" of government, tasked with putting into effect the laws passed by the legislature and running the day-to-day administration of the state.

47. In a presidential system like Nigeria or the USA, the President is...
    A) only Head of State.
    B) only Head of Government.
    C) both Head of State and Head of Government.
    D) a member of the legislature.
    E) appointed by the judiciary.

    **Answer:** C. both Head of State and Head of Government.
    **Explanation:** The President fulfills the ceremonial duties of Head of State (representing the nation) and the political duties of Head of Government (running the government).

48. The collective decision-making body of the executive, consisting of the chief executive and senior ministers, is the...
    A) Civil Service.
    B) Legislature.
    C) Judiciary.
    D) Cabinet.
    E) National Security Council.

    **Answer:** D. Cabinet.
    **Explanation:** The Cabinet is the core group of senior executive officials who head the major government departments and advise the chief executive on policy.

49. In a parliamentary system, the principle of "collective responsibility" means that cabinet members must...
    A) be experts in their field.
    B) publicly support all government decisions or resign.
    C) belong to different political parties.
    D) be elected directly by the people.
    E) answer only to the Head of State.

    **Answer:** B. publicly support all government decisions or resign.
    **Explanation:** Collective responsibility dictates that the cabinet presents a united front to the public and parliament. A minister who cannot publicly support a cabinet decision is expected to resign.

50. The complex system of government departments, agencies, and officials that implement public policy is the...
    A) Legislature.
    B) Judiciary.
    C) Political Party.
    D) Bureaucracy.
    E) Electoral Commission.

    **Answer:** D. Bureaucracy.
    **Explanation:** The bureaucracy, also known as the public administration or civil service, is the operational arm of the executive branch responsible for the day-to-day running of the government.

51. The transfer of powers and resources from central government to elected local governments is known as...
    A) Deconcentration.
    B) Delegation.
    C) Devolution.
    D) Centralization.
    E) Privatization.

    **Answer:** C. Devolution.
    **Explanation:** Devolution is the strongest form of decentralization, involving the transfer of political power, functions, and resources to autonomous, sub-national elected bodies.

52. A key characteristic of an ideal civil service is...
    A) political partisanship.
    B) appointment based on patronage.
    C) temporary tenure.
    D) political neutrality and merit-based recruitment.
    E) election of senior officials.

    **Answer:** D. political neutrality and merit-based recruitment.
    **Explanation:** A professional civil service should be impartial, serving the government of the day regardless of its political affiliation, and its members should be selected and promoted based on competence, not political connections.

53. An independent official who investigates citizens' complaints against government maladministration is the...
    A) Attorney-General.
    B) Auditor-General.
    C) Ombudsman.
    D) Chief Justice.
    E) Speaker of the House.

    **Answer:** C. Ombudsman.
    **Explanation:** The Ombudsman's office (or Public Complaints Commission) acts as an impartial investigator for grievances that citizens have against government agencies, seeking to resolve them and address administrative injustice.

54. Which of the following is a primary function of local government?
    A) Conducting foreign policy.
    B) Printing national currency.
    C) Maintaining the armed forces.
    D) Providing primary education and sanitation services.
    E) Amending the national constitution.

    **Answer:** D. Providing primary education and sanitation services.
    **Explanation:** Local governments are responsible for providing services that directly affect the daily lives of people in their community, such as local roads, waste management, primary health, and primary education.

55. In a parliamentary system, ministers are usually chosen from...
    A) the civil service.
    B) the judiciary.
    C) members of the legislature.
    D) the business community.
    E) the general public through a separate election.

    **Answer:** C. members of the legislature.
    **Explanation:** A key feature of the parliamentary system is the fusion of executive and legislative branches, with the Prime Minister and cabinet ministers being Members of Parliament from the governing party or coalition.

56. The permanent, professional head of a government ministry is typically the...
    A) Minister.
    B) Commissioner.
    C) Permanent Secretary.
    D) Chairperson.
    E) Director.

    **Answer:** C. Permanent Secretary.
    **Explanation:** The Minister is the political head of a ministry, while the Permanent Secretary (or Director-General in some systems) is the most senior career civil servant, responsible for the ministry's administration and providing continuity.

57. The weakest form of decentralization, where administrative tasks are shifted to field offices of the central government, is...
    A) Devolution.
    B) Delegation.
    C) Federalism.
    D) Deconcentration.
    E) Privatization.

    **Answer:** D. Deconcentration.
    **Explanation:** Deconcentration involves moving workload from a central ministry's headquarters to its field offices, but ultimate authority and control remain firmly with the central government.

58. The Auditor-General is responsible for...
    A) prosecuting criminals.
    B) auditing the accounts of government departments and agencies.
    C) advising the president on legal matters.
    D) commanding the police force.
    E) investigating citizen complaints.

    **Answer:** B. auditing the accounts of government departments and agencies.
    **Explanation:** The Auditor-General is an independent oversight official who scrutinizes public spending to ensure financial propriety, accountability, and value for money, reporting their findings to the legislature.

59. Public corporations or parastatals are established by government to...
    A) make laws.
    B) provide specific commercial or social services.
    C) conduct elections.
    D) appoint judges.
    E) oversee the military.

    **Answer:** B. provide specific commercial or social services.
    **Explanation:** Parastatals are government-owned entities created to manage specific sectors or services, often with some degree of operational autonomy (e.g., national power authority, water corporation).

60. Which of these is a mechanism for ensuring legal accountability of the executive?
    A) Elections.
    B) Press conferences.
    C) Judicial review.
    D) Public protests.
    E) Opinion polls.

    **Answer:** C. Judicial review.
    **Explanation:** Judicial review allows courts to examine the legality and constitutionality of executive actions, providing a formal, legal check on executive power and ensuring it operates within the law.

**Chapter 5 & 6: Legislative and Judicial Branches (Combined)**

61. A legislature with two chambers or houses is described as...
    A) Unicameral.
    B) Bicameral.
    C) Multicameral.
    D) Federal.
    E) Unitary.

    **Answer:** B. Bicameral.
    **Explanation:** A bicameral legislature consists of two houses, often an upper house (e.g., Senate) and a lower house (e.g., House of Representatives), as seen in Nigeria and the USA. A unicameral legislature has only one house.

62. The process of dividing a country into electoral districts is known as...
    A) Gerrymandering.
    B) Delimitation.
    C) Referendum.
    D) Plebiscite.
    E) Apportionment.

    **Answer:** B. Delimitation.
    **Explanation:** Delimitation (or districting) is the drawing of boundaries for electoral constituencies. Gerrymandering is the manipulation of these boundaries for political advantage.

63. Which of the following is NOT a primary function of the legislature?
    A) Law-making.
    B) Representation of constituents.
    C) Scrutiny of the executive.
    D) Adjudication of legal disputes.
    E) Control over public finance.

    **Answer:** D. Adjudication of legal disputes.
    **Explanation:** The adjudication of legal disputes is the primary function of the judicial branch (the courts), not the legislature.

64. The presiding officer of the lower house of the legislature (e.g., House of Representatives) is usually the...
    A) President.
    B) Prime Minister.
    C) Chief Justice.
    D) Speaker.
    E) Senate President.

    **Answer:** D. Speaker.
    **Explanation:** The Speaker is the official who presides over debates, maintains order, and manages the business of the lower legislative chamber.

65. The power of the legislature to remove a public official, like a president, from office for gross misconduct is called...
    A) Recall.
    B) Veto.
    C) Impeachment.
    D) Dissolution.
    E) Prorogation.

    **Answer:** C. Impeachment.
    **Explanation:** Impeachment is a formal process by which a legislative body brings charges against a high-ranking public official. It is a powerful check on the executive and judicial branches.

66. A proposed law presented to the legislature for consideration is called a...
    A) Bill.
    B) Motion.
    C) Decree.
    D) By-law.
    E) Clause.

    **Answer:** A. Bill.
    **Explanation:** A bill is a draft of a proposed law that becomes an Act or Statute once it is passed by the legislature and receives executive assent.

67. The highest court in most judicial systems is the...
    A) High Court.
    B) Magistrate Court.
    C) Court of Appeal.
    D) Supreme Court.
    E) Customary Court.

    **Answer:** D. Supreme Court.
    **Explanation:** The Supreme Court (or apex court) is the court of last resort. Its decisions are final and binding on all lower courts.

68. Judicial independence is crucial for...
    A) ensuring the supremacy of the executive.
    B) allowing judges to participate in politics.
    C) upholding the rule of law and ensuring fair trials.
    D) speeding up all legal cases.
    E) helping the legislature to make laws.

    **Answer:** C. upholding the rule of law and ensuring fair trials.
    **Explanation:** An independent judiciary, free from influence by other branches of government, is essential for impartial decision-making, protecting citizens' rights, and maintaining public confidence in the justice system.

69. A legal system that deals with disputes between private individuals or organizations is...
    A) Criminal Law.
    B) Civil Law.
    C) Martial Law.
    D) Customary Law.
    E) Administrative Law.

    **Answer:** B. Civil Law.
    **Explanation:** Civil law governs relationships and disputes between private parties (e.g., contract disputes, property matters, family law), with the aim of providing remedies like compensation. Criminal law deals with offenses against the state.

70. In criminal cases, the burden of proof requires the prosecution to prove guilt...
    A) on the balance of probabilities.
    B) beyond a reasonable doubt.
    C) with absolute certainty.
    D) through a confession.
    E) by a simple majority of evidence.

    **Answer:** B. beyond a reasonable doubt.
    **Explanation:** This is a very high standard of proof required in criminal law to protect the accused from wrongful conviction. The standard in civil cases ("balance of probabilities") is lower.

71. Laws based on the long-established traditions and norms of a particular community are known as...
    A) Statute Law.
    B) Common Law.
    C) Constitutional Law.
    D) Customary Law.
    E) International Law.

    **Answer:** D. Customary Law.
    **Explanation:** Customary law derives its authority from the established customs and practices of a specific ethnic or indigenous community and is often applied in matters of personal status or land.

72. A method of dispute resolution where a neutral third party helps disputing parties reach a non-binding, mutually acceptable agreement is...
    A) Litigation.
    B) Arbitration.
    C) Mediation.
    D) Adjudication.
    E) Prosecution.

    **Answer:** C. Mediation.
    **Explanation:** In mediation, the mediator facilitates negotiation but does not impose a decision. The power to settle rests with the parties themselves. Arbitration, in contrast, involves a neutral party making a binding decision.

73. The special privileges that protect legislators from lawsuits for statements made during official proceedings are known as...
    A) Judicial Immunity.
    B) Executive Privilege.
    C) Diplomatic Immunity.
    D) Parliamentary Privilege.
    E) Habeas Corpus.

    **Answer:** D. Parliamentary Privilege.
    **Explanation:** This privilege, including freedom of speech in debate, is essential for legislators to perform their duties without fear of intimidation or legal action.

74. A court's authority to hear a case for the first time is called...
    A) Appellate Jurisdiction.
    B) Final Jurisdiction.
    C) Original Jurisdiction.
    D) Concurrent Jurisdiction.
    E) Limited Jurisdiction.

    **Answer:** C. Original Jurisdiction.
    **Explanation:** Original jurisdiction is the power to hear a case at its inception. Appellate jurisdiction is the power to review a case already decided by a lower court.

75. The quorum in a legislative house refers to the...
    A) total number of members.
    B) number of members in the majority party.
    C) minimum number of members required to be present for business to be conducted.
    D) number of votes needed to pass a bill.
    E) period for which the house sits.

    **Answer:** C. minimum number of members required to be present for business to be conducted.
    **Explanation:** Without a quorum, any decisions made by the legislature are invalid. It ensures that a small minority cannot pass legislation without sufficient representation.

---

### **Part IV: Electoral Systems and Political Participation**

**Chapter 7: Electoral Processes and Systems**

76. An electoral system where the candidate with the most votes in a constituency wins, even without a majority, is called...
    A) Proportional Representation.
    B) Alternative Vote.
    C) First-Past-the-Post (FPTP).
    D) Two-Round System.
    E) Party-List System.

    **Answer:** C. First-Past-the-Post (FPTP).
    **Explanation:** FPTP, also known as the plurality or winner-takes-all system, awards the seat to the candidate who receives more votes than any other single candidate.

77. A major advantage of the Proportional Representation (PR) electoral system is that it...
    A) always produces stable, single-party governments.
    B) provides a fairer representation of parties' share of the national vote.
    C) is very simple to understand and administer.
    D) creates a strong link between a representative and a specific constituency.
    E) guarantees that the winner has over 50% of the vote.

    **Answer:** B. provides a fairer representation of parties' share of the national vote.
    **Explanation:** The primary goal of PR systems is to allocate legislative seats to parties in proportion to their vote share, thus giving a voice to smaller parties and reducing "wasted votes."

78. The official list of eligible voters used in an election is the...
    A) census register.
    B) voters' register or electoral roll.
    C) party membership list.
    D) national identity card database.
    E) tax roll.

    **Answer:** B. voters' register or electoral roll.
    **Explanation:** A credible and accurate voters' register is fundamental to a fair election, ensuring that only those eligible are able to vote, and only once.

79. The body responsible for organizing, conducting, and supervising elections in a country is the...
    A) Judiciary.
    B) National Assembly.
    C) Electoral Management Body (EMB).
    D) Civil Service Commission.
    E) Ministry of Interior.

    **Answer:** C. Electoral Management Body (EMB).
    **Explanation:** The EMB (e.g., INEC in Nigeria) is the independent institution tasked with managing all aspects of the electoral process to ensure it is free, fair, and credible.

80. A major disadvantage of the First-Past-the-Post system is that it...
    A) leads to unstable coalition governments.
    B) is too complex for voters to understand.
    C) can result in a party winning a majority of seats without a majority of the popular vote.
    D) gives too much power to small parties.
    E) requires multiple rounds of voting.

    **Answer:** C. can result in a party winning a majority of seats without a majority of the popular vote.
    **Explanation:** This disproportionality is a key criticism of FPTP. A party can win many constituencies by narrow margins while the opposition piles up large, "wasted" majorities in other constituencies.

81. Which of the following is a barrier to women's participation in politics?
    A) Constitutional guarantees of equality.
    B) Reserved seats for women.
    C) Socio-cultural norms and patriarchal attitudes.
    D) Free and fair elections.
    E) The existence of multiple political parties.

    **Answer:** C. Socio-cultural norms and patriarchal attitudes.
    **Explanation:** Traditional gender roles and stereotypes often discourage or create obstacles for women seeking to enter the male-dominated field of politics.

82. An electoral system that combines elements of FPTP and PR is known as a...
    A) Direct System.
    B) Unitary System.
    C) Mixed System.
    D) Bicameral System.
    E) Plurality System.

    **Answer:** C. Mixed System.
    **Explanation:** Mixed systems, like Mixed Member Proportional (MMP), attempt to combine the benefits of constituency representation (from FPTP) with the fairness of proportional outcomes (from PR).

83. The secret ballot is important in elections because it...
    A) speeds up the counting process.
    B) ensures voters can choose freely without intimidation or fear.
    C) allows parties to know who voted for them.
    D) prevents anyone under 18 from voting.
    E) makes the election results easy to predict.

    **Answer:** B. ensures voters can choose freely without intimidation or fear.
    **Explanation:** The secrecy of the ballot is a fundamental principle of free elections, protecting voters from coercion and allowing them to express their true preference.

84. The process by which political parties and candidates try to persuade voters is known as...
    A) Registration.
    B) Delimitation.
    C) Adjudication.
    D) Campaigning.
    E) Collation.

    **Answer:** D. Campaigning.
    **Explanation:** Campaigning involves all the activities—rallies, advertising, debates—undertaken to win voter support before an election.

85. Independent domestic and international observers monitor elections to...
    A) determine the winner.
    B) fund the campaigns of candidates.
    C) assess the fairness and credibility of the process.
    D) provide security at polling stations.
    E) count the votes for the electoral commission.

    **Answer:** C. assess the fairness and credibility of the process.
    **Explanation:** Election observers play a crucial watchdog role by observing the process and reporting on its compliance with national laws and international standards, which enhances the legitimacy of the outcome.

**Chapter 8: Political Parties and Interest Groups**

86. A key difference between a political party and a pressure group is that a political party...
    A) has a narrower focus on a single issue.
    B) does not engage in lobbying.
    C) aims to win elections and control government.
    D) is not officially registered.
    E) relies solely on public protests.

    **Answer:** C. aims to win elections and control government.
    **Explanation:** This is the defining characteristic of a political party. Pressure groups, in contrast, seek to influence government policy from the outside without necessarily seeking to form the government themselves.

87. A trade union is an example of a/an...
    A) economic interest group.
    B) single-issue group.
    C) ethnic interest group.
    D) political party.
    E) government agency.

    **Answer:** A. economic interest group.
    **Explanation:** Trade unions (or labor unions) are formed to protect and advance the economic interests of their members (workers), such as wages, working conditions, and job security.

88. The process of influencing government officials to support a group's cause is known as...
    A) Electioneering.
    B) Gerrymandering.
    C) Lobbying.
    D) Filibustering.
    E) Canvassing.

    **Answer:** C. Lobbying.
    **Explanation:** Lobbying involves direct or indirect communication with policymakers to advocate for a particular interest or viewpoint. It is the primary tactic of pressure groups.

89. A pressure group that focuses intensely on one issue, such as environmental protection or gun control, is a...
    A) Professional Association.
    B) Public Interest Group.
    C) Single-Issue Group.
    D) Business Group.
    E) Labor Union.

    **Answer:** C. Single-Issue Group.
    **Explanation:** As the name implies, these groups concentrate their efforts on a narrow range of related issues, about which their members are often very passionate.

90. The media's role in influencing which issues are seen as important by the public and policymakers is called...
    A) propaganda.
    B) agenda-setting.
    C) yellow journalism.
    D) muckraking.
    E) infotainment.

    **Answer:** B. agenda-setting.
    **Explanation:** Agenda-setting theory suggests that by giving more attention to certain issues, the mass media can influence the public's perception of what is important and thus what policymakers should address.

91. Which of the following is NOT a function of political parties?
    A) Recruiting and nominating candidates.
    B) Educating and mobilizing voters.
    C) Formulating policy and programs.
    D) Adjudicating electoral disputes.
    E) Organizing and running government.

    **Answer:** D. Adjudicating electoral disputes.
    **Explanation:** The adjudication of electoral disputes is a judicial function, handled by courts or specialized election tribunals, not by political parties.

92. The "watchdog" role of the media involves...
    A) supporting the government in power at all times.
    B) providing entertainment to the public.
    C) advertising products for businesses.
    D) scrutinizing government actions and exposing corruption.
    E) setting the government's policy agenda.

    **Answer:** D. scrutinizing government actions and exposing corruption.
    **Explanation:** In its watchdog capacity, the media acts as a check on power by investigating and reporting on potential abuses, mismanagement, or wrongdoing, thereby holding officials accountable.

93. The collection of non-governmental and non-profit organizations that operate in the public sphere is known as...
    A) Civil Society.
    B) The State.
    C) The Government.
    D) The Electorate.
    E) The Bureaucracy.

    **Answer:** A. Civil Society.
    **Explanation:** Civil society is the "third sector" of society, distinct from government and business, comprising NGOs, CSOs, community groups, trade unions, and other associations that advance common interests.

94. Laws that regulate the sources and use of money in election campaigns are called...
    A) appropriation laws.
    B) electoral laws.
    C) campaign finance laws.
    D) ethics codes.
    E) lobbying regulations.

    **Answer:** C. campaign finance laws.
    **Explanation:** These laws are designed to promote transparency and limit the potentially corrupting influence of money in politics by setting rules for political donations and spending.

95. An unethical practice in elections where voters are given money or gifts in exchange for their vote is...
    A) lobbying.
    B) campaigning.
    C) canvassing.
    D) vote buying.
    E) filibustering.

    **Answer:** D. vote buying.
    **Explanation:** Vote buying is a form of electoral corruption that undermines the principle of free choice and the integrity of the democratic process.

**Chapter 9: Policy‐Making Process and Implementation**

96. The stage in the policy cycle where problems come to the attention of policymakers is known as...
    A) policy formulation.
    B) policy adoption.
    C) agenda setting.
    D) policy evaluation.
    E) policy implementation.

    **Answer:** C. agenda setting.
    **Explanation:** Agenda setting is the crucial first stage where issues are identified and recognized as worthy of government action, influenced by factors like public opinion, media, and crises.

97. The development of potential solutions or alternative courses of action to address a problem is...
    A) policy implementation.
    B) policy evaluation.
    C) policy adoption.
    D) policy formulation.
    E) agenda setting.

    **Answer:** D. policy formulation.
    **Explanation:** During policy formulation, various actors (bureaucracy, think tanks, interest groups) research and propose different options to solve the identified problem.

98. When a legislature passes a law to address a problem, it is an example of...
    A) policy formulation.
    B) policy adoption.
    C) agenda setting.
    D) policy implementation.
    E) policy evaluation.

    **Answer:** B. policy adoption.
    **Explanation:** Policy adoption is the formal act of choosing and legitimizing a specific policy option, such as through the passage of a bill into law.

99. The primary actor responsible for policy implementation is the...
    A) Legislature.
    B) Judiciary.
    C) Electorate.
    D) Media.
    E) Bureaucracy.

    **Answer:** E. Bureaucracy.
    **Explanation:** The bureaucracy (civil service, government agencies) is responsible for translating the adopted policy into concrete programs and actions.

100. A major challenge to effective policy implementation is...
    A) clear policy objectives.
    B) sufficient resources.
    C) strong political will.
    D) lack of coordination between agencies.
    E) public support.

    **Answer:** D. lack of coordination between agencies.
    **Explanation:** Poor communication and lack of coordination ("silos") between different government departments involved in a policy can severely hinder its effective implementation.

101. The systematic assessment of a policy's effectiveness and impact is called...
    A) policy feedback.
    B) policy formulation.
    C) policy evaluation.
    D) policy adoption.
    E) agenda setting.

    **Answer:** C. policy evaluation.
    **Explanation:** Policy evaluation seeks to determine whether a policy achieved its goals, what its unintended consequences were, and whether it should be continued, modified, or terminated.

102. A "focusing event" like a natural disaster or major crisis can play a significant role in...
    A) policy evaluation.
    B) policy implementation.
    C) agenda setting.
    D) budget allocation.
    E) judicial review.

    **Answer:** C. agenda setting.
    **Explanation:** Major, highly visible events can dramatically elevate an issue's importance and force it onto the government's agenda for immediate action.

103. Which of the following is an example of a Health Policy issue?
    A) Curriculum reform.
    B) Trade liberalization.
    C) Establishing a national health insurance scheme.
    D) Reforming the electoral system.
    E) Building a new highway.

    **Answer:** C. Establishing a national health insurance scheme.
    **Explanation:** Health policy deals with the goals, decisions, and actions undertaken to achieve specific healthcare goals within a society, such as improving access through insurance.

104. Feedback mechanisms in the policy cycle are important because they...
    A) guarantee a policy will be successful.
    B) allow for learning, adaptation, and improvement of policies.
    C) ensure the bureaucracy has total control.
    D) stop the policy process from continuing.
    E) focus only on the initial problem.

    **Answer:** B. allow for learning, adaptation, and improvement of policies.
    **Explanation:** Feedback from evaluations, citizen complaints, and monitoring provides the information needed to make adjustments and improve future policy-making, making the cycle dynamic.

105. The actors who research and draft detailed policy options are often found within the...
    A) general public.
    B) judiciary.
    C) international community.
    D) government agencies and think tanks.
    E) media houses.

    **Answer:** D. government agencies and think tanks.
    **Explanation:** Civil servants within government agencies have the technical expertise, and think tanks provide specialized research, to formulate detailed and evidence-based policy proposals.

**Chapter 10: Human Rights, Civil Liberties, and Social Justice**

106. Inherent, universal, and inalienable rights that all individuals possess are known as...
    A) Civil duties.
    B) Political privileges.
    C) Human rights.
    D) Legal precedents.
    E) Social norms.

    **Answer:** C. Human rights.
    **Explanation:** Human rights are fundamental entitlements that every person has simply by virtue of being human, regardless of their nationality, race, gender, or any other status.

107. The landmark 1948 UN document that proclaimed fundamental rights for all people is the...
    A) Geneva Convention.
    B) UN Charter.
    C) Universal Declaration of Human Rights (UDHR).
    D) International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights.
    E) Kyoto Protocol.

    **Answer:** C. Universal Declaration of Human Rights (UDHR).
    **Explanation:** The UDHR is a foundational document in international human rights law, setting out a common standard of achievement for all nations in the protection of human rights.

108. Freedom from arbitrary government interference is best described as...
    A) a civil duty.
    B) a civil liberty.
    C) social justice.
    D) positive liberty.
    E) economic equality.

    **Answer:** B. a civil liberty.
    **Explanation:** Civil liberties are specific freedoms that protect individuals from unwarranted government action, such as freedom of speech, assembly, and religion. They represent a "negative liberty" (freedom from).

109. Social justice is primarily concerned with...
    A) punishing criminals.
    B) fairness in the distribution of resources, opportunities, and rights.
    C) the separation of powers in government.
    D) the procedures for electing officials.
    E) protecting the state from external threats.

    **Answer:** B. fairness in the distribution of resources, opportunities, and rights.
    **Explanation:** Social justice aims to create an equitable society by addressing systemic inequalities and ensuring all individuals and groups have fair access to societal benefits.

110. The right to freedom of speech is...
    A) absolute and without any limits.
    B) only for journalists and politicians.
    C) a duty, not a right.
    D) often subject to limitations, such as laws against incitement to violence.
    E) only applicable in a monarchy.

    **Answer:** D. often subject to limitations, such as laws against incitement to violence.
    **Explanation:** While a fundamental right, freedom of speech is not absolute. Democratic societies place reasonable limits on it to protect public order, national security, and the rights and reputations of others.

111. Which regional human rights instrument is unique for including peoples' rights and individual duties?
    A) The European Convention on Human Rights.
    B) The American Convention on Human Rights.
    C) The Universal Declaration of Human Rights.
    D) The African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights.
    E) The Arab Charter on Human Rights.

    **Answer:** D. The African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights.
    **Explanation:** The Banjul Charter is distinct in its recognition of collective (peoples') rights, such as the right to development and a satisfactory environment, and its inclusion of duties of the individual towards their family, society, and state.

112. The right to form and join groups like political parties or trade unions is the freedom of...
    A) speech.
    B) assembly.
    C) association.
    D) movement.
    E) religion.

    **Answer:** C. association.
    **Explanation:** Freedom of association is the right of individuals to come together and form groups for common purposes, which is vital for civil society and political participation.

113. An organization like Amnesty International is an example of a...
    A) government agency.
    B) political party.
    C) human rights NGO.
    D) international court.
    E) multinational corporation.

    **Answer:** C. human rights NGO.
    **Explanation:** Amnesty International is a non-governmental organization (NGO) that operates independently of any government to monitor, document, and advocate against human rights abuses worldwide.

114. The concept of "equality of opportunity" means that...
    A) everyone should have the same income and wealth.
    B) all individuals should have an equal chance to succeed, regardless of their background.
    C) only people from certain backgrounds can hold office.
    D) resources should be divided equally among all citizens.
    E) laws apply differently to different groups of people.

    **Answer:** B. all individuals should have an equal chance to succeed, regardless of their background.
    **Explanation:** This principle focuses on providing a level playing field, where success is based on merit and effort, not on factors like race, gender, or family wealth.

115. A National Human Rights Institution (NHRI) is typically established by...
    A) a foreign government.
    B) a private corporation.
    C) the government of a country to promote and protect rights domestically.
    D) the United Nations in every country.
    E) a group of concerned citizens.

    **Answer:** C. the government of a country to promote and protect rights domestically.
    **Explanation:** NHRIs are state-mandated bodies (e.g., a National Human Rights Commission) designed to function as independent watchdogs for human rights within a country's borders.

---

### **Part VI: International Relations and Global Governance**

**Chapter 11: Theories of International Relations**

116. The theory of Realism in international relations assumes that the international system is...
    A) hierarchical.
    B) anarchic.
    C) cooperative.
    D) governed by a world government.
    E) peaceful.

    **Answer:** B. anarchic.
    **Explanation:** A core assumption of Realism is that there is no overarching authority above states in the international system. This anarchy shapes state behavior, forcing them to prioritize survival and power.

117. Which IR theory emphasizes cooperation, international law, and the role of international organizations?
    A) Realism
    B) Liberalism
    C) Marxism
    D) Constructivism
    E) Feminism

    **Answer:** B. Liberalism
    **Explanation:** Liberalism is more optimistic than Realism, arguing that institutions, economic interdependence, and shared democratic values can overcome the challenges of anarchy and foster cooperation.

118. Constructivism argues that state interests and identities are shaped by...
    A) the distribution of military power.
    B) shared ideas, norms, and social interaction.
    C) the dynamics of global capitalism.
    D) innate human nature.
    E) geographical location.

    **Answer:** B. shared ideas, norms, and social interaction.
    **Explanation:** Constructivism's central claim is that the international system is socially constructed. Norms and ideas (like human rights or sovereignty) are powerful forces that shape how states define their interests and behave.

119. A state's supreme and independent authority over its territory is known as...
    A) Power.
    B) Diplomacy.
    C) Sovereignty.
    D) National Interest.
    E) Hegemony.

    **Answer:** C. Sovereignty.
    **Explanation:** Sovereignty is the foundational principle of the modern state system, implying that a state is the ultimate authority within its borders and is not subject to external control.

120. The ability to attract and co-opt rather than coerce, based on the appeal of a country's culture and values, is called...
    A) Hard Power.
    B) Smart Power.
    C) Economic Power.
    D) Military Power.
    E) Soft Power.

    **Answer:** E. Soft Power.
    **Explanation:** Coined by Joseph Nye, soft power is the ability to influence others through attraction and persuasion, using cultural, ideological, or institutional resources.

121. A formal agreement between states to cooperate on security matters is a/an...
    A) Treaty.
    B) Alliance.
    C) Convention.
    D) Protocol.
    E) Diplomacy.

    **Answer:** B. Alliance.
    **Explanation:** An alliance is a specific type of formal agreement where states pledge mutual support, usually military, against a common threat. NATO is a prime example.

122. The "balance of power" theory suggests that states will...
    A) always seek to start wars.
    B) form coalitions to prevent any one state from becoming too dominant.
    C) transfer their sovereignty to a world government.
    D) focus exclusively on economic growth.
    E) dismantle their militaries.

    **Answer:** B. form coalitions to prevent any one state from becoming too dominant.
    **Explanation:** This realist concept posits that states will act (through internal buildup or external alliances) to counter rising powers and maintain a relative equilibrium, thus ensuring their own survival and preventing hegemony.

123. The increasing interconnectedness and interdependence of countries worldwide is known as...
    A) Nationalism.
    B) Regionalism.
    C) Globalization.
    D) Isolationism.
    E) Federalism.

    **Answer:** C. Globalization.
    **Explanation:** Globalization describes the process of intensifying connections across borders in economic, political, cultural, and technological spheres.

124. Marxism as an IR theory focuses on...
    A) the balance of military power.
    B) the role of international norms.
    C) global class struggle and capitalist exploitation.
    D) gender relations in global politics.
    E) the peaceful nature of democracies.

    **Answer:** C. global class struggle and capitalist exploitation.
    **Explanation:** Marxist theories (including World Systems Theory) analyze international relations through an economic lens, emphasizing the inherent inequality and exploitation in the global capitalist system.

125. Which of the following is a political implication of globalization?
    A) A decrease in international trade.
    B) A challenge to state sovereignty.
    C) The decline of international organizations.
    D) The complete elimination of nationalism.
    E) An increase in government control over the economy.

    **Answer:** B. a challenge to state sovereignty.
    **Explanation:** Globalization challenges traditional notions of sovereignty as states must increasingly contend with the influence of international organizations, transnational corporations, global financial flows, and global problems that require collective action.

**Chapter 12: International Organizations and Treaties**

126. The principal organ of the United Nations responsible for maintaining international peace and security is the...
    A) General Assembly.
    B) Security Council.
    C) International Court of Justice.
    D) Economic and Social Council.
    E) Secretariat.

    **Answer:** B. Security Council.
    **Explanation:** The UN Charter gives the Security Council the primary responsibility for peace and security, and it is the only UN body that can make legally binding decisions and authorize the use of force.

127. The "veto power" in the UN Security Council is held by the...
    A) Secretary-General.
    B) ten non-permanent members.
    C) President of the General Assembly.
    D) five permanent members.
    E) judges of the ICJ.

    **Answer:** D. five permanent members.
    **Explanation:** The five permanent members (P5: China, France, Russia, UK, US) have the power to block any substantive resolution with a single "no" vote, a key feature of the UNSC's power structure.

128. The main deliberative and representative organ of the UN, where all member states have an equal vote, is the...
    A) Security Council.
    B) Trusteeship Council.
    C) Secretariat.
    D) General Assembly.
    E) Economic and Social Council.

    **Answer:** D. General Assembly.
    **Explanation:** The General Assembly is the "parliament of nations" where all 193 member states can discuss global issues, though its resolutions are generally non-binding.

129. The African Union (AU) is the successor to the...
    A) Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS).
    B) Organization of African Unity (OAU).
    C) Southern African Development Community (SADC).
    D) League of Arab States.
    E) Non-Aligned Movement.

    **Answer:** B. Organization of African Unity (OAU).
    **Explanation:** The OAU, established in 1963, was restructured and relaunched as the African Union in 2002 with a broader mandate, including peace, security, and integration.

130. A primary goal of ECOWAS is to promote...
    A) military alliances across Africa.
    B) a single currency for the entire world.
    C) cultural exchange with Europe.
    D) economic integration in West Africa.
    E) the independence of all colonies.

    **Answer:** D. economic integration in West Africa.
    **Explanation:** The Economic Community of West African States was founded to foster cooperation and economic integration among its member states, with aims like creating a common market.

131. The legally binding nature of international treaties is based on the principle of...
    A) caveat emptor.
    B) stare decisis.
    C) pacta sunt servanda.
    D) habeas corpus.
    E) ex post facto.

    **Answer:** C. pacta sunt servanda.
    **Explanation:** This Latin phrase, meaning "agreements must be kept," is a fundamental principle of international law, obligating states to honor the treaties they have ratified.

132. The deployment of international military and civilian personnel to a conflict zone with the consent of the parties is known as...
    A) Humanitarian Intervention.
    B) Peacekeeping.
    C) Sanctions.
    D) Diplomacy.
    E) Alliance formation.

    **Answer:** B. Peacekeeping.
    **Explanation:** UN Peacekeeping operations are based on the principles of consent, impartiality, and non-use of force except in self-defense and defense of the mandate.

133. The Paris Agreement is an international treaty that deals with...
    A) human rights.
    B) international trade.
    C) climate change.
    D) nuclear non-proliferation.
    E) the law of the sea.

    **Answer:** C. climate change.
    **Explanation:** The Paris Agreement, under the UNFCCC, is a landmark environmental treaty aiming to limit global warming by having countries commit to reducing their greenhouse gas emissions.

134. A key feature of the European Union (EU) that distinguishes it from most other regional bodies is its...
    A) focus on security only.
    B) supranational institutions and deep level of integration.
    C) exclusion of democratic states.
    D) lack of a court system.
    E) informal, consensus-based decision making.

    **Answer:** B. supranational institutions and deep level of integration.
    **Explanation:** The EU has institutions (like the European Commission and Court of Justice) that can make decisions binding on member states, representing a level of integration and shared sovereignty far beyond most regional organizations.

135. The international norm that a state has a responsibility to protect its population from mass atrocities, and that the international community must act if the state fails, is called...
    A) The Monroe Doctrine.
    B) The Responsibility to Protect (R2P).
    C) The Westphalian Order.
    D) Collective Security.
    E) The Right of Conquest.

    **Answer:** B. The Responsibility to Protect (R2P).
    **Explanation:** R2P is an emerging norm that seeks to reframe the debate on sovereignty and intervention, prioritizing the protection of people from genocide, war crimes, ethnic cleansing, and crimes against humanity.

136. The principal judicial organ of the United Nations is the...
    A) International Criminal Court (ICC).
    B) European Court of Human Rights.
    C) International Court of Justice (ICJ).
    D) African Court on Human and Peoples' Rights.
    E) ECOWAS Community Court.

    **Answer:** C. International Court of Justice (ICJ).
    **Explanation:** The ICJ, or World Court, is one of the six principal organs of the UN and settles legal disputes between states. The ICC is a separate treaty-based court that prosecutes individuals for international crimes.

137. Which of the following is an example of a UN Specialized Agency?
    A) The Security Council.
    B) The General Assembly.
    C) Amnesty International.
    D) The World Health Organization (WHO).
    E) The African Union.

    **Answer:** D. The World Health Organization (WHO).
    **Explanation:** Specialized Agencies are autonomous international organizations with their own charters and budgets, linked to the UN system through ECOSOC. Others include UNESCO, ILO, and FAO.

138. The process of formally approving an international treaty according to a state's constitutional procedures is known as...
    A) Signature.
    B) Negotiation.
    C) Adoption.
    D) Ratification.
    E) Proclamation.

    **Answer:** D. Ratification.
    **Explanation:** Ratification is the crucial step that makes a treaty legally binding on a state. Signature merely indicates an intention to be bound.

139. Post-conflict efforts to rebuild institutions and address the root causes of conflict are termed...
    A) Peacekeeping.
    B) Peacemaking.
    C) Peacebuilding.
    D) Peace enforcement.
    E) Preventive diplomacy.

    **Answer:** C. Peacebuilding.
    **Explanation:** Peacebuilding is a long-term process that occurs after a conflict has ended, aiming to create a sustainable peace and prevent a relapse into violence.

140. A major aim of the World Trade Organization (WTO) is to...
    A) provide development loans to poor countries.
    B) stabilize international exchange rates.
    C) regulate and liberalize international trade.
    D) protect endangered species.
    E) coordinate global health responses.

    **Answer:** C. regulate and liberalize international trade.
    **Explanation:** The WTO provides a framework for negotiating trade agreements and a dispute settlement process for enforcing global trade rules among its members.

---

### **Part VII: Development Studies and Public Administration**

**Chapter 13: Concepts of Development**

141. The concept of "sustainable development" aims to meet the needs of the present without...
    A) relying on foreign aid.
    B) engaging in international trade.
    C) compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs.
    D) increasing a country's GDP.
    E) forming political alliances.

    **Answer:** C. compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs.
    **Explanation:** This is the classic definition from the Brundtland Commission report, highlighting the need to balance economic, social, and environmental considerations for long-term viability.

142. Development measured solely by the growth rate of a country's GDP focuses on...
    A) human development.
    B) sustainable development.
    C) economic growth.
    D) political development.
    E) social development.

    **Answer:** C. economic growth.
    **Explanation:** Economic growth is a narrow measure focusing on the increase in a country's economic output, which is a component of, but not the same as, broader concepts of development.

143. The Human Development Index (HDI) combines measures of...
    A) military strength, political stability, and cultural influence.
    B) life expectancy, education, and standard of living.
    C) GDP growth, inflation, and unemployment.
    D) income inequality, poverty, and infrastructure.
    E) exports, imports, and foreign investment.

    **Answer:** B. life expectancy, education, and standard of living.
    **Explanation:** The HDI provides a more holistic view of development by measuring three key dimensions of human well-being: health (life expectancy), knowledge (schooling), and income (GNI per capita).

144. The GINI coefficient is a measure of...
    A) economic growth.
    B) human development.
    C) environmental quality.
    D) political freedom.
    E) income or wealth inequality.

    **Answer:** E. income or wealth inequality.
    **Explanation:** The GINI coefficient measures the distribution of income within a population. A value of 0 represents perfect equality, while a value of 1 represents perfect inequality.

145. The primary mandate of the International Monetary Fund (IMF) is to...
    A) fund long-term development projects like roads and schools.
    B) ensure the stability of the international monetary system.
    C) promote regional integration in Africa.
    D) regulate global trade in services.
    E) protect human rights worldwide.

    **Answer:** B. ensure the stability of the international monetary system.
    **Explanation:** The IMF acts as a lender of last resort to countries facing balance of payments crises and monitors the global economy to prevent financial instability.

146. Which institution is primarily focused on providing long-term loans and grants for poverty reduction and development projects in the world's poorest countries?
    A) The International Monetary Fund (IMF).
    B) The World Bank Group.
    C) The World Trade Organization (WTO).
    D) The UN Security Council.
    E) The African Development Bank (AfDB).

    **Answer:** B. The World Bank Group.
    **Explanation:** The World Bank's core mission is to fight poverty and support long-term economic development through financial and technical assistance, particularly through its IDA arm for the poorest nations.

147. A major challenge to development in many countries is the lack of adequate...
    A) population.
    B) natural resources.
    C) infrastructure.
    D) national debt.
    E) political ideologies.

    **Answer:** C. infrastructure.
    **Explanation:** A deficit in physical infrastructure—such as roads, electricity, water, and communication networks—is a significant barrier to economic activity, service delivery, and overall development.

148. The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) are a set of...
    A) legally binding military alliances.
    B) trade regulations imposed by the WTO.
    C) 17 global goals adopted by the UN to achieve a better and more sustainable future.
    D) conditions for receiving IMF loans.
    E) theories of international relations.

    **Answer:** C. 17 global goals adopted by the UN to achieve a better and more sustainable future.
    **Explanation:** The SDGs are a universal call to action to end poverty, protect the planet, and ensure that all people enjoy peace and prosperity by 2030.

149. The African Development Bank (AfDB) focuses on providing development finance to...
    A) all developing countries globally.
    B) countries in Asia and Latin America.
    C) countries in the European Union.
    D) countries in Africa.
    E) only the permanent members of the UN Security Council.

    **Answer:** D. countries in Africa.
    **Explanation:** The AfDB is a regional multilateral development bank whose mission is to foster sustainable economic development and social progress in its regional member countries in Africa.

150. A situation where people work in jobs below their skill level or part-time when they desire full-time work is called...
    A) unemployment.
    B) underemployment.
    C) full employment.
    D) structural adjustment.
    E) disguised unemployment.

    **Answer:** B. underemployment.
    **Explanation:** Underemployment describes a situation where the labor of an individual is not fully utilized in terms of skills, hours, or productivity, which is a common problem alongside unemployment.

**Chapter 14: Public Administration and Leadership**

151. Max Weber's ideal-type bureaucracy emphasized all the following EXCEPT...
    A) Hierarchy.
    B) Formal Rules.
    C) Specialization.
    D) Personal Favoritism.
    E) Impersonality.

    **Answer:** D. Personal Favoritism.
    **Explanation:** A key principle of Weber's ideal bureaucracy is impersonality, meaning decisions are based on rules, not personal relationships or favoritism, to ensure fairness and efficiency.

152. A leadership style that focuses on an exchange relationship, using rewards and punishments to motivate followers, is...
    A) Transformational.
    B) Transactional.
    C) Servant.
    D) Laissez-faire.
    E) Autocratic.

    **Answer:** B. Transactional.
    **Explanation:** Transactional leadership is based on a "transaction" where followers are rewarded for meeting clear goals and corrected when they fail. It maintains the status quo effectively.

153. A leader who inspires followers with a compelling vision and challenges them to innovate is demonstrating...
    A) Transactional leadership.
    B) Bureaucratic leadership.
    C) Transformational leadership.
    D) Autocratic leadership.
    E) Trait leadership.

    **Answer:** C. Transformational leadership.
    **Explanation:** Transformational leaders motivate followers to transcend their self-interest for the good of the organization, often through charisma, inspiration, and intellectual stimulation.

154. The abuse of public office for private gain is known as...
    A) Ethics.
    B) Leadership.
    C) Administration.
    D) Corruption.
    E) Reform.

    **Answer:** D. Corruption.
    **Explanation:** This is the standard definition of corruption, which includes acts like bribery, embezzlement, and nepotism.

155. Which of the following is an anti-corruption measure?
    A) Secrecy in government procurement.
    B) Asset declaration by public officials.
    C) Abolishing the judiciary.
    D) Granting immunity to all officials.
    E) Reducing the power of the Auditor-General.

    **Answer:** B. Asset declaration by public officials.
    **Explanation:** Requiring public officials to declare their assets and liabilities upon taking office and periodically thereafter is a key transparency tool to detect and deter illicit enrichment.

156. The use of ICTs by government to provide services and information to citizens is called...
    A) E-commerce.
    B) E-learning.
    C) E-government.
    D) E-mail.
    E) E-banking.

    **Answer:** C. E-government.
    **Explanation:** E-government involves using technology to improve the efficiency, accessibility, and transparency of government operations and service delivery.

157. The ethical principle that requires public officials to make decisions based on merit, without bias or favoritism, is...
    A) Integrity.
    B) Accountability.
    C) Transparency.
    D) Objectivity (Impartiality).
    E) Diligence.

    **Answer:** D. Objectivity (Impartiality).
    **Explanation:** Objectivity is crucial for fairness and public trust, ensuring that decisions are not influenced by personal feelings, biases, or relationships.

158. A leadership style where the leader puts the needs of others first and focuses on their growth and well-being is...
    A) Autocratic leadership.
    B) Transactional leadership.
    C) Trait leadership.
    D) Servant leadership.
    E) Bureaucratic leadership.

    **Answer:** D. Servant leadership.
