Explanation: Lower Courts, such as Magistrate Courts, are described as the entry point for less serious civil and criminal matters.

73. The primary function of a Court of Appeal is to
(A) conduct new trials
(B) hear cases for the first time
(C) review decisions from lower courts for errors of law
(D) sentence offenders

Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that Courts of Appeal "review the record of the lower court proceedings to determine if errors of law or procedure occurred."

74. The principle that the judiciary should be free from improper influence from other branches of government is
(A) Judicial review
(B) Judicial activism
(C) Judicial independence
(D) Judicial precedent

Answer: C
Explanation: Judicial Independence is defined as "the principle that the judiciary should be free from improper influence from the other branches of government."

75. Appointing judges for life or until a mandatory retirement age promotes judicial independence by ensuring
(A) financial security
(B) security of tenure
(C) political influence
(D) popular support

Answer: B
Explanation: The text explains that Security of Tenure for judges is a key mechanism to ensure judicial independence, as they cannot be easily removed for unpopular decisions.

76. Law that deals with offenses committed against the state or society as a whole is
(A) Civil law
(B) Customary law
(C) Criminal law
(D) Administrative law

Answer: C
Explanation: Criminal Law is defined as dealing "with offenses committed against the state or society as a whole (crimes)."

77. In a criminal case, the standard of proof required for a conviction is
(A) on the balance of probabilities
(B) beyond a reasonable doubt
(C) preponderance of the evidence
(D) clear and convincing evidence

Answer: B
Explanation: The text specifies that in criminal law, guilt must be proven "beyond a reasonable doubt," which is a high standard.

78. A legal dispute between two private individuals over a contract is an example of a
(A) criminal case
(B) civil case
(C) constitutional case
(D) customary case

Answer: B
Explanation: Civil Law is described as dealing with "disputes between private individuals, organizations, or entities," with contract disputes listed as an example.

79. Laws based on the long-established traditions and social norms of a particular community are known as
(A) Statutory law
(B) Common law
(C) Customary law
(D) Constitutional law

Answer: C
Explanation: Customary Law is defined as "Laws, rules, and practices that are based on the long-established customs, traditions, and social norms of a particular indigenous community."

80. A legal system derived from Islamic religious sources is known as
(A) Canon law
(B) Sharia law
(C) Common law
(D) Civil law

Answer: B
Explanation: The text defines Sharia Law as "A legal system derived from Islamic religious sources, primarily the Quran and the Sunnah."

81. Which of the following is a method of Alternative Dispute Resolution (ADR)?
(A) Litigation
(B) Prosecution
(C) Mediation
(D) Judgment

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists Mediation as a key method of Alternative Dispute Resolution, alongside arbitration and negotiation.

82. A key difference between arbitration and mediation is that an arbitrator's decision is
(A) non-binding
(B) always appealed
(C) legally binding
(D) only advisory

Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that an arbitrator "make[s] a binding decision (an award)," while a mediator helps parties reach a voluntary agreement.

83. In mediation, the role of the mediator is to
(A) impose a solution on the parties
(B) represent one of the parties
(C) facilitate communication to help parties reach an agreement
(D) punish the party at fault

Answer: C
Explanation: The text describes the mediator's role as facilitating "communication, clarif[ying] issues, [and] explor[ing] options" but not making a binding decision.

84. A court with the power to hear a case for the first time has
(A) appellate jurisdiction
(B) original jurisdiction
(C) final jurisdiction
(D) concurrent jurisdiction

Answer: B
Explanation: The text explains that Original Jurisdiction is the power "to hear certain types of cases directly for the first time."

85. The application of Sharia law in some Nigerian states is primarily in the area of
(A) international trade
(B) corporate law
(C) personal status law
(D) electoral law

Answer: C
Explanation: The text notes that Sharia law is often applied to "personal status law (marriage, divorce, inheritance) for Muslims."

86. The main advantage of ADR over court litigation is that it is often
(A) more formal and rigid
(B) more expensive and slower
(C) quicker and less adversarial
(D) open to the public

Answer: C
Explanation: The text highlights that ADR processes "are often quicker, less expensive, more flexible, and less adversarial than going to court."

87. A court that deals with cases involving minors is a
(A) Small Claims Court
(B) Juvenile Court
(C) Tax Court
(D) Customary Court

Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists Juvenile Courts as courts that "Deal with cases involving minors."

88. A key function of the Supreme Court is to
(A) handle minor traffic offenses
(B) serve as the final court of appeal
(C) prosecute criminals
(D) draft legislation

Answer: B
Explanation: The Supreme Court's primary function is described as "Final appellate jurisdiction," making its decisions final and binding.

89. The requirement that judges' salaries should not be arbitrarily reduced by the executive is a measure to ensure their
(A) political loyalty
(B) public popularity
(C) financial security and independence
(D) accountability to the legislature

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists "Financial Security" as a mechanism to ensure judicial independence, protecting judges from financial pressure.

90. A dispute between two states in a federation like Nigeria would likely be heard first by the
(A) Magistrate Court
(B) High Court
(C) Court of Appeal
(D) Supreme Court

Answer: D
Explanation: The text notes that the Supreme Court may have original jurisdiction in certain cases, such as "disputes between states in a federation."

Chapter 7: Electoral Processes and Systems (20 Questions)

91. An electoral system where the candidate with the most votes wins the seat, even without a majority, is called
(A) Proportional Representation
(B) First-Past-the-Post
(C) Alternative Vote
(D) Two-Round System

Answer: B
Explanation: The text defines the First-Past-the-Post (FPTP) system as one where the candidate with a plurality (the most votes) wins the seat.

92. A major disadvantage of the First-Past-the-Post system is that it
(A) always leads to coalition governments
(B) is too complex for voters to understand
(C) can lead to disproportionate representation
(D) gives smaller parties an advantage

Answer: C
Explanation: A key disadvantage listed in the text is that FPTP "Often results in disproportionate representation," where a party's seat share doesn't match its vote share.

93. Proportional Representation systems aim to allocate seats to parties based on their
(A) number of candidates
(B) campaign spending
(C) share of the national vote
(D) historical performance

Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that Proportional Representation (PR) systems "aim to allocate seats to political parties in direct proportion to their share of the national vote."

94. A common outcome of Proportional Representation systems is
(A) single-party majority governments
(B) two-party dominance
(C) coalition governments
(D) the elimination of small parties

Answer: C
Explanation: The text notes that PR "Often leads to coalition governments, which can be less stable or take longer to form."

95. A system that requires a candidate to win more than 50% of the vote is a
(A) Plurality system
(B) Majority system
(C) Proportional system
(D) List system

Answer: B
Explanation: Majority Systems, such as the Two-Round System, are defined by the requirement that a "candidate must win an absolute majority (more than 50%) of votes."

96. The official list of eligible citizens who can vote in an election is the
(A) Party manifesto
(B) Constitution
(C) Voters' register
(D) Ballot paper

Answer: C
Explanation: Voter registration is the process of enrolling citizens on an "official list (voters' register or electoral roll)."

97. The independent body responsible for organizing and supervising elections in a country is the
(A) Judiciary
(B) Executive
(C) Electoral Management Body
(D) Civil Service

Answer: C
Explanation: The Electoral Management Body (EMB), such as an Electoral Commission, is "responsible for organizing, conducting, and supervising elections."

98. Delimitation of electoral constituencies refers to the process of
(A) registering voters
(B) counting votes
(C) drawing electoral boundaries
(D) accrediting observers

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists "Delimitation of electoral constituencies (boundaries)" as a key function of an EMB.

99. The observation of an election by independent domestic and international groups is called
(A) Election campaigning
(B) Election monitoring
(C) Election rigging
(D) Election petitioning

Answer: B
Explanation: Election Monitoring is defined as "The observation of an election process by independent domestic and/or international observers."

100. A key purpose of voter registration is to
(A) ensure only the ruling party's supporters can vote
(B) prevent multiple voting
(C) raise funds for political parties
(D) determine the winner of the election

Answer: B
Explanation: The text states that a purpose of voter registration is to "prevent multiple voting."

101. A major barrier to women's participation in politics mentioned in the text is
(A) lack of interest in politics
(B) constitutional prohibitions
(C) socio-cultural norms and patriarchal attitudes
(D) higher levels of education

Answer: C
Explanation: The text explicitly lists "Socio-cultural norms and patriarchal attitudes" as a key barrier to women's participation in politics.

102. Reserving seats in the legislature for women is a strategy known as
(A) gerrymandering
(B) recall
(C) quotas
(D) referendum

Answer: C
Explanation: The text mentions "Quotas (reserved seats or party list quotas) for women in legislatures" as a strategy to enhance women's participation.

103. In a mixed electoral system like Mixed Member Proportional (MMP), voters typically cast
(A) one vote for a party
(B) one vote for a candidate
(C) two votes: one for a candidate and one for a party
(D) multiple votes for several candidates

Answer: C
Explanation: The text explains that in MMP systems, "Voters typically have two votes: one for a local constituency candidate (FPTP) and one for a party list (PR)."

104. The Two-Round System is designed to ensure the winner has
(A) a plurality of votes
(B) the support of the military
(C) an absolute majority of votes
(D) the most campaign funds

Answer: C
Explanation: The Two-Round System is a type of majority system, ensuring the eventual winner secures over 50% of the vote in the final round.

105. All of the following are functions of an Electoral Management Body (EMB) EXCEPT
(A) voter registration
(B) appointing cabinet ministers
(C) civic and voter education
(D) announcing official election results

Answer: B
Explanation: Appointing cabinet ministers is a function of the chief executive (President or Prime Minister), not the EMB. The other options are core functions of an EMB as listed in the text.

110. One of the main reasons for youth disengagement from politics is the
(A) lack of complex political issues
(B) perception of politics as corrupt
(C) over-representation of youth in government
(D) simplicity of joining political parties

Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists the "Perception of politics as corrupt or dominated by older generations" as a barrier to youth participation.

107. The period during which political parties try to persuade voters to support them is known as
(A) polling
(B) registration
(C) campaigning
(D) adjudication

Answer: C
Explanation: Political campaigning is defined as "The process by which political parties and candidates try to persuade voters to support them in an election."

108. The secrecy of the ballot is essential to ensure
(A) high voter turnout
(B) free choice for voters
(C) a quick counting process
(D) that only educated people vote

Answer: B
Explanation: The text highlights that "Secrecy of Ballot" is "Essential for free choice."

109. An electoral system that combines elements of FPTP and PR is known as a
(A) unitary system
(B) mixed system
(C) pure system
(D) direct system

Answer: B
Explanation: Mixed Electoral Systems are defined as those that "Combine elements of both plurality/majority systems... and proportional representation systems."

110. A major advantage of the PR system is that it
(A) creates stable, single-party governments
(B) provides fairer representation for smaller parties
(C) has a strong constituency-representative link
(D) is very simple to administer

Answer: B
Explanation: The text states that a key advantage of PR is "Better representation for smaller parties and minority groups."

Chapter 8: Political Parties and Interest Groups (20 Questions)

111. An organized group that seeks to control government by winning elections is a
(A) Pressure group
(B) Civil society organization
(C) Political party
(D) Government agency

Answer: C
Explanation: A Political Party is defined as an organized group that "seek[s] to influence or control government policy and personnel by nominating and electing candidates to public office."

112. When a political party develops a set of proposals to address societal problems, it is performing the function of
(A) policy formulation
(B) interest aggregation
(C) political recruitment
(D) election monitoring

Answer: A
Explanation: The text lists "Formulate Policies and Programs (Party Platform/Manifesto)" as a key function of political parties.

113. An organized group that seeks to influence public policy on a specific issue without seeking office is an
(A) electoral commission
(B) interest group
(C) opposition party
(D) executive cabinet

Answer: B
Explanation: An Interest Group (or Pressure Group) is defined as a group that "seek[s] to influence public policy and government decisions... without necessarily seeking to gain control of government themselves."

114. Chambers of Commerce and trade unions are examples of
(A) single-issue interest groups
(B) social interest groups
(C) economic interest groups
(D) ethnic interest groups

Answer: C
Explanation: The text categorizes Business Groups (Chambers of Commerce) and Labor Unions (Trade Unions) under Economic Interest Groups.

115. An interest group that focuses intensely on one specific issue, such as gun control or environmental protection, is a
(A) professional association
(B) single-issue group
(C) labor union
(D) business group

Answer: B
Explanation: Single-Issue Interest Groups are defined as those that "Focus intensely on one specific issue or a narrow set of related issues."

116. The process of making direct contact with policymakers to influence their decisions is called
(A) litigation
(B) electioneering
(C) grassroots mobilization
(D) lobbying

Answer: D
Explanation: The text describes Direct Lobbying as "Direct contact with policymakers... to present information, arguments, and advocate for their position."

117. The media's role in investigating and exposing corruption in government is known as its
(A) agenda-setting role
(B) watchdog role
(C) socialization role
(D) entertainment role

Answer: B
Explanation: The text describes the Watchdog Role of the media as "Investigating and exposing corruption, mismanagement, or abuses of power by government officials."

118. The "third sector" of society, distinct from government and business, is referred to as
(A) the judiciary
(B) the opposition
(C) civil society
(D) the bureaucracy

Answer: C
Explanation: Civil Society is defined in the text as "the 'third sector' of society, distinct from government (state) and business (market)."

119. Which of the following is a component of civil society?
(A) The Presidency
(B) The National Assembly
(C) Non-Governmental Organizations (NGOs)
(D) The Central Bank

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists NGOs as a key component of Civil Society Organizations (CSOs).

120. Regulations on campaign finance often include
(A) requirements for unlimited spending
(B) a ban on all individual donations
(C) limits on contributions from individuals or organizations
(D) mandatory donations from corporations

Answer: C
Explanation: The text mentions that campaign finance regulations may include "Limits on contributions from individuals or organizations."

121. The use of state resources by an incumbent for campaigning is considered an
(A) ethical practice
(B) unethical practice
(C) electoral reform
(D) acceptable strategy

Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists "misuse of state resources for campaigning" as an example of an unethical practice.

122. An interest group using lawsuits to challenge laws or policies is engaging in
(A) litigation
(B) direct lobbying
(C) protest
(D) electioneering

Answer: A
Explanation: The text lists Litigation, or using "lawsuits and court action to challenge laws or policies," as a method used by interest groups.

123. The media's ability to influence which issues are seen as important by the public is its
(A) framing function
(B) watchdog function
(C) agenda-setting function
(D) partisan function

Answer: C
Explanation: The agenda-setting function of the media is described as "Influencing which issues are perceived as important by the public and policymakers."

124. When an interest group mobilizes its members to write letters and make calls to policymakers, it is using
(A) direct lobbying
(B) grassroots lobbying
(C) litigation
(D) a media blackout

Answer: B
Explanation: Grassroots Lobbying is defined as "Mobilizing their members and the public to contact policymakers."

125. A key difference between a political party and an interest group is that a political party
(A) focuses only on a single issue
(B) avoids influencing government
(C) seeks to win elections and control government
(D) is not formally organized

Answer: C
Explanation: The text distinguishes parties from interest groups by stating that parties "seek to win elections and control government," while interest groups primarily aim to influence policy.

126. Bringing together diverse interests from various segments of society into a coherent policy platform is called
(A) interest articulation
(B) interest aggregation
(C) political recruitment
(D) judicial review

Answer: B
Explanation: The text defines Interest Aggregation as the function of "Bring[ing] together diverse interests... and mold[ing] them into a broader, more coherent set of policy proposals."

127. The funding of electoral campaigns by parties and candidates is known as
(A) campaign finance
(B) public administration
(C) fiscal policy
(D) party manifesto

Answer: A
Explanation: Campaign Finance is defined in the text as "The funding of electoral campaigns by political parties, candidates, and supporting groups."

128. An advertisement that attacks an opponent's character or record is a
(A) positive ad
(B) negative ad
(C) comparison ad
(D) issue-focused ad

Answer: B
Explanation: The text defines negative ads as those that "attack opponent's character, record, or policies."

129. The spread of misinformation and "fake news" is a major challenge associated with
(A) traditional print media
(B) public service broadcasting
(C) social media
(D) direct lobbying

Answer: C
Explanation: The text notes that the rise of social media presents challenges like "spread of misinformation/disinformation - 'fake news'."

130. A professional association, such as a medical or bar association, is a type of
(A) economic interest group
(B) ethnic interest group
(C) single-issue group
(D) public interest group

Answer: A
Explanation: The text classifies Professional Associations under the category of Economic Interest Groups.

Chapter 9: Policy‐Making Process and Implementation (15 Questions)

131. The actions and decisions adopted by a government to address specific problems are known as
(A) Public opinion
(B) Public policy
(C) Public relations
(D) Public administration

Answer: B
Explanation: Public policy is defined in the introduction as "the actions, decisions, and regulations adopted by a government... to address specific problems."

132. The first stage in the policy-making cycle is
(A) policy adoption
(B) policy implementation
(C) agenda setting
(D) policy evaluation

Answer: C
Explanation: The text outlines the policy cycle, starting with Agenda Setting (Problem Identification and Definition).

133. When an issue comes to the attention of policymakers as requiring government action, it has entered the
(A) implementation stage
(B) evaluation stage
(C) political agenda
(D) feedback loop

Answer: C
Explanation: Agenda Setting is the process by which "problems or issues come to the attention of policymakers and are recognized as requiring government action."

134. The stage where potential solutions or alternative courses of action are developed is
(A) policy formulation
(B) policy adoption
(C) agenda setting
(D) policy evaluation

Answer: A
Explanation: Policy Formulation is defined as "The development of potential solutions or alternative courses of action (policy options)."

135. When a policy is formally chosen and given legal force, for example by passing a law, it is in the stage of
(A) policy formulation
(B) policy adoption
(C) policy implementation
(D) policy review

Answer: B
Explanation: Policy Adoption is the stage where "a specific policy option is formally chosen and authorized by a legitimate government body."

136. The primary actors responsible for carrying out and putting a policy into action are the
(A) Judiciary
(B) Legislature
(C) Bureaucracy
(D) Interest groups

Answer: C
Explanation: The text identifies the Bureaucracy (Civil Service, Government Agencies) as the "Primary implementers" of policy.

137. Which of the following is a major challenge to effective policy implementation?
(A) Clear policy objectives
(B) Sufficient resources
(C) Strong political will
(D) Poor communication between agencies

Answer: D
Explanation: The text lists "Poor communication and coordination between agencies" as a significant administrative challenge to policy implementation.

138. The systematic assessment of the effectiveness and impact of a public policy is called
(A) policy formulation
(B) policy advocacy
(C) policy evaluation
(D) agenda setting

Answer: C
Explanation: Policy Evaluation is defined as "The systematic assessment of the effectiveness, efficiency, and impact of a public policy."

139. A key purpose of policy evaluation is to
(A) guarantee funding for all policies
(B) provide evidence for continuing, modifying, or terminating a policy
(C) ensure the policy is popular with the public
(D) punish civil servants for failures

Answer: B
Explanation: The text states that a purpose of evaluation is "to provide evidence for making decisions about whether to continue, modify, expand, or terminate a policy."

140. When information about policy performance is used to inform future decisions, this is part of the
(A) adoption mechanism
(B) lobbying process
(C) feedback mechanism
(D) electoral campaign

Answer: C
Explanation: Feedback Mechanisms are described as processes where "information about policy performance... is collected and fed back into the policy-making system to inform future decisions."

141. A major crisis, like a public health emergency, is often referred to as a
(A) policy outcome
(B) focusing event
(C) feedback loop
(D) bureaucratic problem

Answer: B
Explanation: The text mentions that "Crises and Focusing Events" can thrust issues onto the policy agenda.

142. The process of translating broad policy statements into specific operational procedures is a key activity in
(A) agenda setting
(B) policy implementation
(C) lobbying
(D) electioneering

Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists "Developing Rules, Regulations, and Guidelines" as a key activity in policy implementation.

143. Which type of policy evaluation assesses how a policy is being carried out and if activities are being conducted as planned?
(A) Impact Evaluation
(B) Process Evaluation
(C) Cost-Benefit Analysis
(D) Outcome Evaluation

Answer: B
Explanation: Process Evaluation (or Implementation Evaluation) is defined as assessing "how a policy is being implemented."

144. In the policy-making process, think tanks and academic experts primarily contribute during the
(A) implementation stage
(B) formulation stage
(C) adoption stage
(D) termination stage

Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists "Think tanks and academic experts" as actors involved in Policy Formulation, where they "conduct research and propose policy alternatives."

145. "What happens after a bill becomes a law" is a simple way to describe policy
(A) evaluation
(B) formulation
(C) adoption
(D) implementation

Answer: D
Explanation: The text uses this exact phrase to introduce the concept of Policy Implementation.

Chapter 10: Human Rights, Civil Liberties, and Social Justice (15 Questions)

146. Inherent, universal, and inalienable entitlements that all individuals possess are known as
(A) Human rights
(B) Civil duties
(C) Political powers
(D) Social contracts

Answer: A
Explanation: The text defines Human Rights as "Inherent, universal, inalienable, and indivisible rights that all individuals possess simply by virtue of being human."

147. The landmark document adopted by the UN in 1948, proclaiming fundamental rights for all, is the
(A) Magna Carta
(B) Bill of Rights
(C) Universal Declaration of Human Rights (UDHR)
(D) African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights

Answer: C
Explanation: The text identifies the Universal Declaration of Human Rights (UDHR) as the "landmark document" adopted in 1948.

148. The concept of 'negative liberty' refers to
(A) freedom to achieve one's potential
(B) freedom from external interference
(C) the denial of freedom
(D) the duty to be free

Answer: B
Explanation: Negative Liberty is defined as "Freedom from external interference, constraints, or oppression."

149. The idea that all individuals should have an equal chance to succeed in life, regardless of background, is
(A) equality of outcome
(B) equality of opportunity
(C) formal equality
(D) natural equality

Answer: B
Explanation: Equality of Opportunity is defined as the principle that "All individuals should have an equal chance to succeed in life, regardless of their background."

150. Fairness in the allocation of resources, benefits, and burdens in society is the focus of
(A) retributive justice
(B) procedural justice
(C) restorative justice
(D) distributive justice

Answer: D
Explanation: The text defines Distributive Justice as "Fairness in the allocation of resources, benefits, and burdens in society."

151. Which international treaty specifically focuses on civil and political rights like freedom of speech and the right to a fair trial?
(A) ICESCR
(B) ICCPR
(C) CEDAW
(D) CRC

Answer: B
Explanation: The text explains that the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR) focuses on rights like freedom of speech and fair trial.

152. A unique feature of the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights is its recognition of
(A) only individual rights
(B) only peoples' rights
(C) both individual and peoples' rights, as well as duties
(D) only economic rights

Answer: C
Explanation: The text states the African Charter is "Unique in that it recognizes not only individual rights but also peoples' rights... and individual duties."

153. Freedom of speech is not absolute and can be limited by laws against
(A) political criticism
(B) unpopular opinions
(C) religious expression
(D) defamation and incitement to violence

Answer: D
Explanation: The text notes that freedom of speech can be limited by laws against things like "defamation" and "incitement to violence."

154. The right of individuals to gather together peacefully in groups is the freedom of
(A) speech
(B) association
(C) religion
(D) assembly

Answer: D
Explanation: Freedom of Assembly is defined as "The right of individuals to gather together peacefully in groups."

155. An independent public body established by a government to promote and protect human rights within a country is a
(A) Non-Governmental Organization (NGO)
(B) National Human Rights Institution (NHRI)
(C) Political Action Committee (PAC)
(D) Transnational Corporation (TNC)

Answer: B
Explanation: The text describes National Human Rights Institutions (NHRIs) as "Independent public bodies established by government... with a mandate to promote and protect human rights."

156. Amnesty International and Human Rights Watch are examples of
(A) government agencies
(B) international human rights NGOs
(C) national human rights institutions
(D) intergovernmental organizations

Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists Amnesty International and Human Rights Watch as examples of "International Human Rights NGOs."

157. The main role of human rights NGOs is to
(A) prosecute criminals
(B) pass legislation
(C) monitor and document abuses, and advocate for change
(D) command military forces

Answer: C
Explanation: The text highlights key roles of human rights NGOs as "Monitoring and Documenting Abuses" and "Advocacy and Lobbying."

158. The right to form and join groups like political parties or trade unions is the freedom of
(A) expression
(B) assembly
(C) association
(D) movement

Answer: C
Explanation: Freedom of Association is defined as "The right of individuals to form and join groups or organizations."

159. The two main legally binding treaties that form the International Bill of Human Rights along with the UDHR are the
(A) Geneva Conventions
(B) ICCPR and ICESCR
(C) Rome Statute and Paris Agreement
(D) CEDaw and CRC

Answer: B
Explanation: The text identifies the ICCPR and ICESCR as the two key legally binding treaties that, with the UDHR, form the "International Bill of Human Rights."

160. Fairness in the processes and procedures used to make decisions and administer laws is called
(A) social justice
(B) procedural justice
(C) distributive justice
(D) retributive justice

Answer: B
Explanation: Procedural Justice is defined as "Fairness in the processes and procedures used to make decisions and administer laws."

Chapter 11: Theories of International Relations (20 Questions)

161. The study of interactions between states and other actors in the international system is
(A) Public Administration
(B) International Relations
(C) Comparative Politics
(D) Political Philosophy

Answer: B
Explanation: International Relations (IR) is defined in the introduction as "the study of the interactions between states... in the international system."

162. Which IR theory assumes the international system is anarchic and states are primarily motivated by survival and power?
(A) Liberalism
(B) Constructivism
(C) Realism
(D) Marxism

Answer: C
Explanation: The core assumptions of Realism listed in the text include an anarchic system, states as primary actors, and the primary goal being survival and security through power.

163. The idea that economic interdependence and international institutions can foster cooperation is central to
(A) Realism
(B) Liberalism
(C) Marxism
(D) Anarchism

Answer: B
Explanation: The text states that Liberalism emphasizes how "Shared interests, economic interdependence, democratic values, and international institutions can foster cooperation."

164. The theory that focuses on how shared ideas, norms, and identities shape state behavior is
(A) Realism
(B) Liberalism
(C) Constructivism
(D) Marxism

Answer: C
Explanation: Constructivism's core assumption is that "The international system and state interests are... socially constructed through interactions, shared ideas, norms, identities, and discourse."

165. Which theory views international relations as a struggle between a dominant capitalist 'core' and an exploited 'periphery'?
(A) Feminism
(B) Realism
(C) Liberalism
(D) Marxism

Answer: D
Explanation: Marxism and its related theories focus on an "unequal division of wealth and power between dominant core... states and exploited periphery... states."

166. "Anarchy is what states make of it" is a key phrase associated with
(A) Realism
(B) Liberalism
(C) Constructivism
(D) Feminism

Answer: C
Explanation: The text directly quotes this phrase from Alexander Wendt as a central idea of Constructivism.

167. The supreme and independent authority of a state over its territory is known as
(A) Power
(B) Sovereignty
(C) National Interest
(D) Diplomacy

Answer: B
Explanation: Sovereignty is defined as "The supreme and independent authority of a state over its territory and population."

1-68. The ability of a state to influence others to achieve its goals is called
(A) Sovereignty
(B) Diplomacy
(C) Power
(D) Alliance

Answer: C
Explanation: Power is defined as "The ability of an actor (e.g., a state) to influence the behavior of other actors to achieve its own goals."

169. The goals and objectives that guide a state's foreign policy are its
(A) National Interest
(B) Constitution
(C) Legal System
(D) Social Contract

Answer: A
Explanation: National Interest is defined as "The goals, objectives, and ambitions of a state in its interactions with other states."

170. According to Joseph Nye, the ability to attract and co-opt rather than coerce is
(A) Hard Power
(B) Smart Power
(C) Soft Power
(D) Military Power

Answer: C
Explanation: The text defines Soft Power, a concept from Joseph Nye, as the "Ability to attract and co-opt rather than coerce."

171. A formal agreement between states to cooperate on matters of mutual security is an
(A) Alliance
(B) Ideology
(C) Interest Group
(D) International Law

Answer: A
Explanation: An Alliance is defined as a "Formal agreements or coalitions between two or more states to cooperate on matters of mutual security or defense."

172. NATO is an example of a
(A) trade bloc
(B) cultural organization
(C) defensive alliance
(D) single-issue interest group

Answer: C
Explanation: The text uses NATO as the prime example of a Defensive Alliance.

173. The concept that states will form coalitions to prevent any single state from becoming too dominant is called
(A) Collective security
(B) Balance of power
(C) National interest
(D) Globalization

Answer: B
Explanation: Balance of Power is described as a situation where states "strive to ensure that no single state or bloc becomes overwhelmingly powerful."

174. The increasing interconnectedness and interdependence between countries worldwide is known as
(A) Nationalism
(B) Isolationism
(C) Globalization
(D) Regionalism

Answer: C
Explanation: Globalization is defined as a "process characterized by increasing interconnectedness and interdependence between countries and societies worldwide."

175. A major political implication of globalization is the
(A) strengthening of state sovereignty
(B) decline of international organizations
(C) challenge to state sovereignty
(D) decrease in global trade

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists "Challenges to State Sovereignty" as a key political implication of globalization.

176. Which theory would be most interested in analyzing how gender roles influence concepts like war and peace?
(A) Realism
(B) Liberalism
(C) Marxism
(D) Feminist IR Theory

Answer: D
Explanation: Feminist IR Theory is described as examining "how gender... shapes international relations" and influences concepts like "war, peace, security, power."

177. The principle that other states should not interfere in the domestic affairs of a sovereign state is
(A) non-intervention
(B) collective security
(C) balance of power
(D) internationalism

Answer: A
Explanation: The text lists Non-intervention as a key aspect of Westphalian sovereignty.

178. The art and practice of conducting negotiations and managing relations between states is
(A) War
(B) Sanctions
(C) Diplomacy
(D) Espionage

Answer: C
Explanation: Diplomacy is defined as "The art and practice of conducting negotiations and managing relations between states."

179. A world dominated by two major powers, such as during the Cold War, is described as
(A) Unipolar
(B) Bipolar
(C) Multipolar
(D) Nonpolar

Answer: B
Explanation: The text refers to the Cold War as an example of a bipolar balance of power.

180. A realist would argue that the most important national interest is
(A) promoting human rights
(B) economic prosperity
(C) survival and security
(D) cultural exchange

Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that for Realists, "Survival and Security" is the primary national interest.

Chapter 12: International Organizations and Treaties (20 Questions)

181. The United Nations was established in
(A) 1919
(B) 1939
(C) 1945
(D) 1960

Answer: C
Explanation: The text states the UN was "established in 1945 (after World War II)."

182. Which principal organ of the UN has all member states represented with one vote each?
(A) Security Council
(B) General Assembly
(C) ECOSOC
(D) International Court of Justice

Answer: B
Explanation: The text specifies that in the General Assembly, "All member states have equal representation (one state, one vote)."

183. The UN organ with primary responsibility for maintaining international peace and security is the
(A) General Assembly
(B) Secretariat
(C) Security Council
(D) ECOSOC

Answer: C
Explanation: The Security Council is described as having "Primary responsibility for maintaining international peace and security."

184. The veto power in the UN Security Council is held by the
(A) ten non-permanent members
(B) Secretary-General
(C) five permanent members
(D) President of the General Assembly

Answer: C
Explanation: The text clarifies that the five permanent members (P5) "have veto power."

185. The principal judicial organ of the UN is the
(A) International Criminal Court (ICC)
(B) International Court of Justice (ICJ)
(C) European Court of Human Rights
(D) African Court on Human and Peoples' Rights

Answer: B
Explanation: The text identifies the International Court of Justice (ICJ) as "The principal judicial organ of the UN."

186. The successor to the Organization of African Unity (OAU) is the
(A) African Union (AU)
(B) ECOWAS
(C) European Union (EU)
(D) African Development Bank (AfDB)

Answer: A
Explanation: The text states the African Union (AU) was "established in 2002 (successor to Organization of African Unity - OAU)."

187. ECOWAS is a regional organization focused on
(A) military alliance in East Africa
(B) cultural exchange in North Africa
(C) political union in Southern Africa
(D) economic integration in West Africa

Answer: D
Explanation: ECOWAS is described as a "Regional economic integration organization for West African countries."

188. A unique feature of the European Union (EU) compared to other regional bodies is its
(A) focus on security only
(B) deep level of supranational integration
(C) small number of member states
(D) lack of a common market

Answer: B
Explanation: The text describes the EU as having a "deep level of integration, with supranational institutions and common policies."

189. A formal, legally binding written agreement between states governed by international law is a
(A) resolution
(B) declaration
(C) treaty
(D) custom

Answer: C
Explanation: International Treaties are defined as "Formal, legally binding written agreements between states."

190. The principle that agreements must be kept is known in international law as
(A) jus cogens
(B) stare decisis
(C) pacta sunt servanda
(D) habeas corpus

Answer: C
Explanation: The text states, "'Pacta sunt servanda' (agreements must be kept) is a fundamental principle of international law."

191. The Paris Agreement is an international treaty that primarily addresses
(A) human rights
(B) international trade
(C) climate change
(D) laws of the sea

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists the Paris Agreement under the UNFCCC as a key treaty dealing with the environment, aiming "to limit global warming."

192. The deployment of international military and civilian personnel to a conflict area with the consent of the parties is called
(A) humanitarian intervention
(B) peacekeeping
(C) invasion
(D) sanctions

Answer: B
Explanation: Peacekeeping Operations are defined as the "Deployment of international... personnel to areas of conflict, with the consent of host state(s)."

193. The use of military force in a state without its consent to stop widespread human rights violations is
(A) Peacekeeping
(B) Diplomacy
(C) Humanitarian intervention
(D) Peacebuilding

Answer: C
Explanation: Humanitarian Intervention is defined as "The use of military force by external actors in a state without its consent to prevent or stop widespread and grave violations of human rights."

194. The emerging international norm that states have a duty to protect their populations from mass atrocities is the
(A) Responsibility to Protect (R2P)
(B) Balance of Power
(C) Democratic Peace Theory
(D) Westphalian Sovereignty

Answer: A
Explanation: The text defines the Responsibility to Protect (R2P) as the norm asserting that states have a primary responsibility to protect their populations from genocide, war crimes, etc.

195. Which UN organ oversees the work of specialized agencies like WHO and UNESCO?
(A) Security Council
(B) General Assembly
(C) Secretariat
(D) ECOSOC

Answer: D
Explanation: The Economic and Social Council (ECOSOC) is described as overseeing "work of many UN specialized agencies, funds, and programs."

196. The administrative head of the United Nations is the
(A) Secretary-General
(B) President of the General Assembly
(C) Chief Prosecutor of the ICJ
(D) High Commissioner for Human Rights

Answer: A
Explanation: The text identifies the Secretary-General as the head of the Secretariat and the "chief administrative officer" of the UN.

197. The process by which a state gives its formal approval to be legally bound by a treaty is called
(A) signature
(B) adoption
(C) negotiation
(D) ratification

Answer: D
Explanation: Ratification is defined as the "formal approval by a state according to its constitutional procedures, making it legally binding."

198. ECOMOG is the historical peacekeeping force of which organization?
(A) AU
(B) EU
(C) ECOWAS
(D) UN

Answer: C
Explanation: The text mentions ECOMOG as the historical peacekeeping force of ECOWAS.

199. The "ASEAN Way" of decision-making emphasizes
(A) majority voting
(B) consensus-building
(C) judicial rulings
(D) unilateral action

Answer: B
Explanation: The text states that ASEAN "Focuses on consensus-based decision-making ('ASEAN Way')."

200. The Geneva Conventions are a set of international treaties concerning
(A) environmental protection
(B) international trade rules
(C) the laws of war
(D) the non-proliferation of nuclear weapons

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists the Geneva Conventions as treaties dealing with the "laws of war, protection of civilians and prisoners of war."

Chapter 13: Concepts of Development (20 Questions)

201. Development that focuses on increasing a country's output of goods and services is called
(A) Sustainable development
(B) Human development
(C) Economic growth
(D) Political development

Answer: C
Explanation: The text defines Economic Growth as focusing "primarily on the increase in a country's output of goods and services."

202. The most common measure of economic growth is the
(A) Human Development Index (HDI)
(B) GINI Coefficient
(C) Gross Domestic Product (GDP)
(D) Consumer Price Index (CPI)

Answer: C
Explanation: Economic growth is "typically measured by the growth rate of Gross Domestic Product (GDP)."

203. A key limitation of using GDP as a measure of development is that it does not account for
(A) total production
(B) market value
(C) income inequality
(D) exports

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists "Income inequality and distribution of wealth" as a key limitation of GDP as a sole measure of development.

204. The development approach that focuses on expanding people's choices and capabilities is
(A) Economic growth
(B) Sustainable development
(C) Human development
(D) Neocolonialism

Answer: C
Explanation: Human Development is defined as an approach "that focuses on expanding people's choices, capabilities, and freedoms."

205. The Human Development Index (HDI) combines measures of life expectancy, standard of living, and
(A) political freedom
(B) education
(C) environmental quality
(D) military strength

Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists the three dimensions of HDI as a long and healthy life (life expectancy), being knowledgeable (education), and a decent standard of living (GNI).

206. Development that "meets the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs" is
(A) Human development
(B) Sustainable development
(C) Economic development
(D) Uneven development

Answer: B
Explanation: The text provides this exact definition from the Brundtland Commission for Sustainable Development.

207. The three integrated dimensions of sustainable development are economic, environmental, and
(A) political
(B) social
(C) military
(D) cultural

Answer: B
Explanation: The text states that sustainable development integrates three dimensions: Economic, Social, and Environmental.

208. A measure of income inequality within a nation is the
(A) HDI
(B) GDP
(C) GINI Coefficient
(D) CPI

Answer: C
Explanation: The GINI Coefficient is defined as "A measure of income inequality or wealth inequality."

209. A GINI coefficient of 0 represents
(A) perfect inequality
(B) perfect equality
(C) high poverty
(D) high development

Answer: B
Explanation: The text explains that a GINI of 0 signifies "Perfect equality (everyone has the same income/wealth)."

210. Which international financial institution's primary mandate is to ensure the stability of the international monetary system?
(A) World Bank
(B) African Development Bank
(C) International Monetary Fund (IMF)
(D) World Trade Organization

Answer: C
Explanation: The IMF's primary mandate is described as ensuring "the stability of the international monetary system."

211. The World Bank's main focus is on
(A) short-term balance of payments support
(B) global military security
(C) long-term economic development and poverty reduction
(D) setting global trade rules

Answer: C
Explanation: The text states the World Bank's main focus is "on long-term economic development and poverty reduction in developing countries."

212. The policy reforms that countries must undertake to receive loans from the IMF are known as
(A) grants
(B) conditionalities
(C) treaties
(D) development goals

Answer: B
Explanation: The text explains that IMF loans "often come with conditionalities (policy reforms the country must undertake)."

213. A regional multilateral institution that provides loans and grants for development projects in Africa is the
(A) World Bank
(B) IMF
(C) African Development Bank (AfDB)
(D) European Investment Bank

Answer: C
Explanation: The text defines the African Development Bank (AfDB) as a "regional multilateral development finance institution... to promote the economic development and social progress of African countries."

214. Which of the following is a major challenge to development in many developing countries?
(A) High levels of education
(B) Strong infrastructure
(C) Political stability
(D) High unemployment

Answer: D
Explanation: The text lists "Unemployment and Underemployment" as a key challenge to development.

215. The lack of adequate roads, railways, and electricity supply is known as an
(A) infrastructure deficit
(B) institutional deficit
(C) governance deficit
(D) educational deficit

Answer: A
Explanation: Infrastructure Deficit is defined as the "Lack of adequate and reliable physical infrastructure."

216. The International Development Association (IDA) is the part of the World Bank Group that provides
(A) loans to middle-income countries
(B) investment guarantees
(C) interest-free loans and grants to the poorest countries
(D) arbitration services

Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that the IDA "Provides interest-free loans (credits) and grants to the world's poorest countries."

217. The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) provide a global framework for development to be achieved by
(A) 2025
(B) 2030
(C) 2040
(D) 2050

Answer: B
Explanation: The text mentions that the SDGs provide a framework for achieving sustainable development "by 2030."

218. Amartya Sen is a key economist associated with the concept of
(A) economic growth
(B) human development
(C) structural adjustment
(D) dependency theory

Answer: B
Explanation: The text credits economists like Amartya Sen with pioneering the Human Development approach.

219. The "trickle-down effect" is an assumption most closely associated with the
(A) human development model
(B) sustainable development model
(C) economic growth model
(D) dependency model

Answer: C
Explanation: The text notes that the economic growth model "Assumes that economic growth will lead to improved living standards ('trickle-down effect')."

220. The Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) measures poverty based on
(A) income alone
(B) consumption alone
(C) inequality
(D) deprivations in health, education, and living standards

Answer: D
Explanation: The MPI is described as measuring poverty "based on deprivations in multiple dimensions (health, education, living standards) rather than just income."

Chapter 14: Public Administration and Leadership (20 Questions)

221. The implementation of government policy and the academic discipline that studies it is known as
(A) Political Science
(B) Public Administration
(C) Sociology
(D) International Relations

Answer: B
Explanation: The chapter introduction defines Public Administration as "the implementation of government policy and the academic discipline that studies this implementation."

222. Excessive adherence to complex rules that leads to delays is a bureaucratic dysfunction known as
(A) goal displacement
(B) red tape
(C) impersonality
(D) hierarchy

Answer: B
Explanation: The text defines Red Tape as "Excessive adherence to complex rules and procedures, leading to delays and inefficiency."

223. The leadership theory that assumes leaders are born with certain innate qualities is the
(A) Trait Theory
(B) Behavioral Theory
(C) Contingency Theory
(D) Servant Leadership

Answer: A
Explanation: Trait Theory is described as assuming "leaders are born with certain innate qualities or traits that make them effective."

224. A leadership style that focuses on an exchange relationship, using rewards and punishments to motivate followers, is
(A) Transformational
(B) Transactional
(C) Servant
(D) Laissez-faire

Answer: B
Explanation: Transactional Leadership is defined as focusing "on the exchange relationship between leaders and followers... using rewards... and punishments."

225. A leader who inspires followers to achieve extraordinary outcomes by articulating a compelling vision is described as
(A) Transactional
(B) Autocratic
(C) Transformational
(D) Bureaucratic

Answer: C
Explanation: Transformational Leadership focuses on "inspiring and motivating followers to achieve extraordinary outcomes," often through "Inspirational Motivation."

226. The leadership style that prioritizes the growth and well-being of people and communities is
(A) Trait Leadership
(B) Transactional Leadership
(C) Servant Leadership
(D) Autocratic Leadership

Answer: C
Explanation: Servant Leadership is described as focusing "primarily on the growth and well-being of people and the communities to which they belong."

227. The abuse of public office for private gain is called
(A) Ethics
(B) Accountability
(C) Corruption
(D) Transparency

Answer: C
Explanation: Corruption is defined as "The abuse of entrusted power or public office for private gain."

228. Showing undue favor to relatives in appointments is a form of corruption known as
(A) Bribery
(B) Extortion
(C) Fraud
(D) Nepotism

Answer: D
Explanation: The text lists Nepotism as "Showing undue favor to relatives or friends in appointments or contracts."

229. Independent bodies like Nigeria's EFCC and ICPC are examples of
(A) Anti-Corruption Agencies
(B) Electoral Management Bodies
(C) National Human Rights Institutions
(D) Local Government Councils

Answer: A
Explanation: The text provides these as examples of "Independent Anti-Corruption Agencies (ACAs)."

230. A key anti-corruption measure that protects individuals who report wrongdoing is
(A) asset declaration
(B) codes of conduct
(C) whistleblower protection
(D) freedom of information

Answer: C
Explanation: Whistleblower Protection is listed as a key anti-corruption measure for "Protecting individuals who report corruption."

231. The use of ICTs by government to provide services and information to citizens is
(A) E-commerce
(B) E-learning
(C) E-government
(D) E-mail

Answer: C
Explanation: E-Government is defined as "The use of Information and Communication Technologies (ICTs) by government agencies to provide information and services to citizens."

232. A major challenge to implementing e-government is the
(A) lack of paper
(B) digital divide
(C) simplicity of government processes
(D) over-abundance of cybersecurity experts

Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists the "Digital divide (unequal access to ICTs)" as a key challenge for e-government.

233. The systematic process of planning, monitoring, and evaluating the performance of government organizations is
(A) public relations
(B) performance management
(C) election monitoring
(D) political campaigning

Answer: B
Explanation: Performance Management in Public Sector is defined as "A systematic process of planning, monitoring, evaluating, and improving the performance of government organizations."

234. Broad efforts to change the structures and processes of public administration to improve performance are known as
(A) political revolutions
(B) constitutional amendments
(C) administrative reforms
(D) judicial reviews

Answer: C
Explanation: Administrative Reforms are defined as "Broad efforts to change the structures, processes, and culture of public administration."

235. The ethical principle that requires public officials to make decisions based on merit, without bias, is
(A) Integrity
(B) Accountability
(C) Objectivity
(D) Diligence

Answer: C
Explanation: Objectivity (or Impartiality) is defined as "Making decisions based on merit, evidence, and law, without bias, favoritism, or prejudice."

236. The New Public Management (NPM) approach to reform sought to introduce
(A) more government control
(B) traditional bureaucratic methods
(C) market-based principles into the public sector
(D) a single global government

Answer: C
Explanation: New Public Management (NPM) is described as "Introducing market-based principles and private sector management techniques into public sector."

237. When a public official's personal interests conflict with their official duties, this is a
(A) conflict of interest
(B) case of fraud
(C) form of extortion
(D) breach of contract

Answer: A
Explanation: The text defines Conflict of Interest as a situation "When a public official's personal interests conflict with their official duties."

238. Making government budgets and procurement processes open to public scrutiny enhances
(A) secrecy
(B) corruption
(C) confidentiality
(D) transparency

Answer: D
Explanation: The text lists this as a key example of a measure to improve Transparency and fight corruption.

239. The idea that effective leadership depends on the context and the followers is central to
(A) Trait theories
(B) Contingency theories
(C) Behavioral theories
(D) Transactional theories

Answer: B
Explanation: Contingency (or Situational) Theories "Argue that effective leadership depends on the situation."

240. When a bureaucracy focuses on following rules for their own sake rather than achieving policy goals, it is known as
(A) red tape
(B) hierarchy
(C) goal displacement
(D) impersonality

Answer: C
Explanation: Goal Displacement is defined as "Focusing on rules and procedures for their own sake rather than achieving the original policy goals."

Chapter 15: Structure and Functions of Local Government (10 Questions)

241. The principle that public functions should be exercised by the lowest level of government competent to perform them is
(A) Sovereignty
(B) Subsidiarity
(C) Centralization
(D) Separation of Powers

Answer: B
Explanation: The text defines Subsidiarity as "the principle that public functions should be exercised by the lowest level of government competent to perform them effectively."

242. The legislative and policy-making body at the local level is the
(A) Local Council
(B) Traditional Council
(C) Executive Committee
(D) Town Clerk's Office

Answer: A
Explanation: The Local Council is described as "The primary legislative and policy-making body at the local level."

243. The head of the executive arm of a local government is often a
(A) Councillor
(B) Chairperson or Mayor
(C) Town Clerk
(D) Magistrate

Answer: B
Explanation: The text identifies the "Chairperson/Mayor/Council Leader" as the head of the local executive.

244. In many African countries, traditional authorities like chiefs and emirs play a role in
(A) conducting foreign policy
(B) commanding the national army
(C) dispute resolution using customary methods
(D) managing the central bank

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists "Dispute resolution (using customary methods)" as a potential role for traditional authorities.

245. A major source of internally generated revenue for local governments is
(A) grants from the central government
(B) international aid
(C) property rates and taxes
(D) loans from the IMF

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists Property rates/taxes as a key example of "Locally Generated Revenue."

246. Grants from a central government that must be used for a particular sector, like primary education, are called
(A) general purpose grants
(B) specific purpose grants
(C) equalization grants
(D) unconditional grants

Answer: B
Explanation: Specific Purpose Grants are defined as those that "Must be used for particular sectors or programs."

247. The financial plan for a local government, outlining expected revenues and proposed expenditures, is the
(A) Manifesto
(B) Constitution
(C) Budget
(D) Development Plan

Answer: C
Explanation: The text defines a Local Government Budget as "A financial plan for a fiscal year, outlining expected revenues and proposed expenditures."

248. Spending by a local government on the construction of a new school or road is an example of
(A) recurrent expenditure
(B) capital expenditure
(C) consumption expenditure
(D) debt servicing

Answer: B
Explanation: Capital Expenditure is defined as "Investment in long-term assets (e.g., construction of schools, roads...)."

249. Participatory budgeting and community scorecards are mechanisms for promoting
(A) centralization
(B) community participation
(C) corruption
(D) national security

Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists "Participatory budgeting processes" and "Community scorecards" as mechanisms for Community Participation.

250. Improving access to roads, electricity, and water in non-urban areas is a key aspect of
(A) foreign policy
(B) urban renewal
(C) rural development
(D) international trade

Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists "Infrastructure Development: Improving access to roads, electricity, water..." as a key aspect of Rural Development.
